=== [02:17] 1급으로 청의가 되는 법은 ===
meaning: The way to become a first-class Cheong-i is...
tone: Informal, explanatory, slightly playful.
--- word ---
term: 1급으로
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 1급 + -(으)로
meaning: as a first-class
notes: 1급 (ilgeup) means 'first class' or 'grade 1'. The particle -(으)로 (-(eu)ro) indicates a qualification, status, or means. Here, it means 'as a first-class [Cheong-i]'.
--- word ---
term: 청의가
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 청의 + -가
meaning: Cheong-i (subject)
notes: 청의 (Cheong-i) is Hwasa's affectionate term for her fans, as noted in the Story Bible. -가 (-ga) is a subject particle, indicating that 'Cheong-i' is the subject of the clause.
--- word ---
term: 되는
type: verb ending
breakdown: 되다 + -는
meaning: becoming
notes: 되다 (doeda) means 'to become'. -는 (-neun) is a present tense adnominal ending, turning the verb into a descriptive modifier for the following noun (법, beop - way/method).
--- word ---
term: 법은
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 법 + -은
meaning: the way (topic)
notes: 법 (beop) means 'way' or 'method'. -은 (-eun) is a topic particle, marking 'the way' as the topic of the sentence. The pattern -는 법 (-neun beop) means 'the way to do X'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)로 (-(eu)ro)
meaning: Indicates a qualification, status, or role. It can be translated as 'as a...' or 'in the capacity of...'.
example: 선생님으로 일해요. (Seonsaengnim-euro ilhaeyo.) - I work as a teacher.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는 법 (-(eu)n/neun beop)
meaning: Means 'the way to do something' or 'how to do something'. -는 is used for verbs, -(으)ㄴ for adjectives.
example: 한국어를 배우는 법 (Hanguk-eoreul baeuneun beop) - The way to learn Korean.
level: Intermediate

=== [02:46] 나한테 맛있는 걸 주는 뇌물을 ===
meaning: to give me delicious things as a bribe.
tone: Informal, demanding, playful.
--- word ---
term: 나한테
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + -한테
meaning: to me
notes: 나 (na) is the informal first-person pronoun 'I'. -한테 (-hante) is an informal particle meaning 'to' or 'for' a person or animal. It's commonly used in casual conversation. The more formal/neutral equivalent is -에게 (-ege).
--- word ---
term: 맛있는
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 맛있다 + -는
meaning: delicious
notes: 맛있다 (masitta) means 'to be delicious'. -는 (-neun) is an adnominal ending that modifies a noun, creating 'delicious things'.
--- word ---
term: 걸
type: noun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 + -을
meaning: the thing (object)
notes: 걸 (geol) is a common contraction of 것 (geot, 'thing') and the object particle -을 (-eul). This contraction is very frequent in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 주는
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 주다 + -는
meaning: giving
notes: 주다 (juda) means 'to give'. -는 (-neun) is a present tense adnominal ending, modifying the following noun (뇌물, noemul - bribe) to mean 'a bribe that gives'.
--- word ---
term: 뇌물을
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 뇌물 + -을
meaning: bribe (object)
notes: 뇌물 (noemul) means 'bribe'. -을 (-eul) is an object particle, marking 'bribe' as the direct object of the implied verb 'to give'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -한테 (-hante)
meaning: A particle used after a person or animal to indicate the recipient of an action, meaning 'to' or 'for'. It is informal.
example: 친구한테 말했어요. (Chinguhante maraesseoyo.) - I told my friend.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는 것 (-(eu)n/neun geot)
meaning: This pattern nominalizes a verb or adjective phrase, turning it into 'the thing that is/does X'. -는 is used for present tense verbs, -(으)ㄴ for past tense verbs or adjectives.
example: 내가 좋아하는 것 (Naega johahaneun geot) - The thing I like.
level: Intermediate

=== [02:51] 맞아. 사내진미를 나에게 바쳐야 해. ===
meaning: Right. You have to offer me the finest delicacies.
tone: Informal, assertive, playful.
--- word ---
term: 맞아.
type: adjective (informal)
breakdown: 맞다
meaning: Right.
notes: 맞다 (matda) means 'to be correct' or 'to be right'. 맞아 (maja) is its informal, casual form, often used as a conversational interjection to agree with someone or affirm a statement.
--- word ---
term: 사내진미를
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 사내진미 + -를
meaning: the finest delicacies (object)
notes: 사내진미 (sanaejinmi) refers to 'delicacies from mountains and seas,' meaning the finest or rarest foods, as per the Story Bible. -를 (-reul) is an object particle.
--- word ---
term: 나에게
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + -에게
meaning: to me
notes: 나 (na) is 'I' (informal). -에게 (-ege) is a particle meaning 'to' or 'for' a person or animal. It is generally more formal or neutral than -한테 (-hante), though both are used for recipients. Hwasa uses both in quick succession, perhaps for stylistic variation or emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 바쳐야
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 바치다 + -아야/어야
meaning: must offer
notes: 바치다 (bachida) means 'to offer' or 'to dedicate'. -아야/어야 (-aya/eoya) is a connective ending that, when combined with 하다 (hada, 'to do'), forms the grammar pattern -아야/어야 하다, meaning 'must' or 'have to'.
--- word ---
term: 해.
type: verb (informal)
breakdown: 하다
meaning: do / have to
notes: 해 (hae) is the informal present tense form of 하다 (hada, 'to do'). Here, it completes the -아야/어야 하다 pattern, meaning 'you have to offer'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아야/어야 하다 (-aya/eoya hada)
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, meaning 'must' or 'have to'. The choice between -아야 and -어야 depends on the vowel of the preceding verb stem.
example: 공부해야 해요. (Gongbuhaeya haeyo.) - I have to study.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -한테 (-hante) vs. -에게 (-ege)
meaning: Both particles mean 'to' or 'for' a person or animal. -한테 is more informal and common in spoken language, while -에게 is more formal or neutral and can be used in both spoken and written contexts.
example: 엄마한테 전화했어요. (Eommahante jeonhwahaesseoyo.) - I called Mom. / 친구에게 편지를 썼어요. (Chinguege pyeonjireul sseosseoyo.) - I wrote a letter to my friend.
level: Beginner

=== [03:24] 이게 맛이 진짜 많거든? ===
meaning: There are really so many flavors of this, you know?
tone: Informal, conversational, slightly rhetorical.
--- word ---
term: 이게
type: pronoun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 이것 + -이
meaning: This (subject)
notes: 이게 (ige) is a common contraction of 이것이 (igeosi), meaning 'this (subject)'. 이것 (igeot) means 'this thing', and -이 (-i) is a subject particle.
--- word ---
term: 맛이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 맛 + -이
meaning: flavor (subject)
notes: 맛 (mat) means 'flavor' or 'taste'. -이 (-i) is a subject particle. The phrase 맛이 많다 (masi manta) means 'to have many flavors' or 'to be very tasty'.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, truly
notes: 진짜 (jinjja) is a common colloquial adverb meaning 'really' or 'truly'. It can also be used as an exclamation of surprise or agreement.
--- word ---
term: 많거든?
type: adjective + ending + question mark
breakdown: 많다 + -거든 + ?
meaning: there are many, you know?
notes: 많다 (manta) means 'to be many' or 'to be a lot'. -거든 (-geodeun) is a conversational ending used to provide background information or a reason, often with a nuance of 'you know' or 'as you know'. The question mark implies seeking agreement or confirmation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -거든 (-geodeun)
meaning: A conversational ending used to provide a reason or background information, often implying 'because...' or 'you know...'. It can also be used to express a condition ('if/when...').
example: 피곤하거든 일찍 자요. (Piggonhageodeun iljjik jayo.) - If you're tired, sleep early. / 이거 맛있거든. (Igeo masitgeodeun.) - This is delicious, you know.
level: Intermediate

=== [03:28] 하나하나 다 먹으려니 안 되니까 ===
meaning: Since I can't eat them all one by one...
tone: Informal, reflective, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 하나하나
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: one by one, each
notes: 하나하나 (hanahana) is a reduplication of 하나 (hana, 'one'), meaning 'one by one' or 'each and every one'. It emphasizes the individual nature of the items.
--- word ---
term: 다
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: all, completely
notes: 다 (da) is an adverb meaning 'all' or 'completely'. It indicates that the action applies to every item or to the full extent.
--- word ---
term: 먹으려니
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 먹다 + -(으)려니
meaning: since I intend to eat
notes: 먹다 (meokda) means 'to eat'. -(으)려니 (-(eu)ryeoni) is a connective ending that expresses 'since one intends to do X' or 'as one is about to do X', often followed by a consequence or realization. Here, it implies 'since I'm trying to eat them all...'
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is a short-form negative adverb placed directly before a verb or adjective to negate it, meaning 'not'.
--- word ---
term: 되니까
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 되다 + -(으)니까
meaning: because it doesn't work / isn't possible
notes: 되다 (doeda) means 'to be possible' or 'to work'. -(으)니까 (-(eu)nikka) is a reason/cause ending, meaning 'because' or 'since'. So, 'because it's not possible' or 'because it won't work'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)려니 (-(eu)ryeoni)
meaning: Indicates that the speaker is about to do something or intends to do something, and a consequence or realization follows. It can be translated as 'as I was about to...', 'since I intended to...', or 'thinking that I would...'.
example: 밥을 먹으려니 전화가 왔어요. (Babeul meogeuryeoni jeonhwaga wasseoyo.) - As I was about to eat, the phone rang.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)니까 (-(eu)nikka)
meaning: Expresses a reason or cause, meaning 'because' or 'since'. It often implies that the reason is a new discovery or a direct consequence.
example: 비가 오니까 우산이 필요해요. (Biga onikka usani piryohaeyo.) - Because it's raining, I need an umbrella.
level: Beginner

=== [03:33] 매니저님 몇 개만 주고 내가 다 가져가야겠다. 그치? ===
meaning: I'll give a few to the manager and take all the rest. Right?
tone: Informal, decisive, seeking agreement, playful.
--- word ---
term: 매니저님
type: noun + honorific
breakdown: 매니저 + -님
meaning: Manager (honorific)
notes: 매니저 (maenijeo) means 'manager'. -님 (-nim) is a general honorific suffix used to show respect to a person, often attached to titles or names. Hwasa uses it for her manager.
--- word ---
term: 몇
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: a few, some
notes: 몇 (myeot) is a determiner used before a counter noun to ask 'how many' or to indicate 'a few' or 'some' unspecified quantity.
--- word ---
term: 개만
type: counter + particle
breakdown: 개 + -만
meaning: only a few (items)
notes: 개 (gae) is a common counter for general items. -만 (-man) is a particle meaning 'only' or 'just', emphasizing exclusivity.
--- word ---
term: 주고
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 주다 + -고
meaning: give and
notes: 주다 (juda) means 'to give'. -고 (-go) is a connective ending that links two or more actions in sequence, meaning 'and' or 'and then'.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + -가
meaning: I (subject)
notes: 내가 (naega) is the informal first-person pronoun 'I' (나, na) combined with the subject particle -가 (-ga). This is the correct form when 나 is the subject.
--- word ---
term: 다
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: all, completely
notes: 다 (da) is an adverb meaning 'all' or 'completely', indicating that the speaker will take everything.
--- word ---
term: 가져가야겠다.
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 가져가다 + -아야/어야겠다
meaning: I guess I should take / I will have to take
notes: 가져가다 (gajyeogada) means 'to take away'. -아야/어야겠다 (-aya/eoyagetda) is a combination of the necessity ending -아야/어야 하다 and the intention/conjecture ending -겠다. It expresses the speaker's resolution, intention, or a strong feeling that they 'should' or 'will have to' do something.
--- word ---
term: 그치?
type: adjective (contraction) + question mark
breakdown: 그렇다 + -지 + ?
meaning: Right?
notes: 그치? (geuchi?) is a common informal contraction of 그렇지? (geureochi?), which comes from 그렇다 (geureota, 'to be so') + -지 (-ji, a confirmatory ending). It's used to seek agreement or confirmation, meaning 'Right?' or 'Isn't it?'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고 (-go)
meaning: Connects two or more verbs or clauses, indicating a sequence of actions or a simple conjunction ('and', 'and then').
example: 밥을 먹고 커피를 마셨어요. (Babeul meokgo keopireul masyeosseoyo.) - I ate and then drank coffee.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아야/어야겠다 (-aya/eoyagetda)
meaning: Expresses the speaker's strong intention, resolution, or a realization that they 'must' or 'should' do something. It's a combination of obligation (-아야/어야 하다) and intention/conjecture (-겠다).
example: 이제 집에 가야겠어요. (Ije jibe gayagesseoyo.) - I guess I should go home now.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -지? (-ji?)
meaning: A conversational ending used to seek confirmation or agreement from the listener, often translated as 'Right?' or 'Isn't it?'. It implies the speaker expects agreement.
example: 이거 맛있지? (Igeo masitji?) - This is delicious, right?
level: Beginner

=== [04:46] 맛있었지? ===
meaning: It was delicious, right?
tone: Casual, seeking agreement, reminiscent.
--- word ---
term: 맛있었지
type: Adjective + past tense + interrogative ending
breakdown: 맛있다 (to be delicious) + -었- (past tense) + -지 (interrogative ending)
meaning: It was delicious, right?
notes: 맛있다 (masitta) means 'to be delicious' and is an adjective. -었- (-eot-) is the past tense suffix. -지 (-ji) is a casual interrogative ending used to confirm something the speaker believes to be true or to seek agreement, similar to 'isn't it?' or 'right?'. It is an informal (반말, banmal) ending. A common learner mistake is confusing -지 with -죠 (jyo), which is the polite (존댓말, jondaetmal) form of the same ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/Adj-었/았지?
meaning: Used to confirm a past event or state that the speaker believes to be true or to seek agreement from the listener. Implies 'It was [V/Adj], wasn't it?'
example: 재미있었지? (It was fun, wasn't it?)
level: Beginner

=== [04:46] 원 없이 먹었어, 오늘도. ===
meaning: I ate to my heart's content, today too.
tone: Casual, satisfied, reflective.
--- word ---
term: 원
type: Noun
breakdown: 원
meaning: wish, desire
notes: In this context, '원' refers to one's desire or wish. It's often used in expressions like '원 없이' (won eop-si). This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 없이
type: Adverb
breakdown: 없다 (to not exist) + -이 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: without, to one's heart's content (in this phrase)
notes: The base form is 없다 (eopda), meaning 'to not exist' or 'to not have.' The suffix -이 (-i) turns the adjective/verb into an adverb. '원 없이' (won eop-si) is an idiom meaning 'without limit/regret' or 'to one's heart's content,' implying doing something as much as one wants without any lack or dissatisfaction. This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 먹었어
type: Verb + past tense + informal ending
breakdown: 먹다 (to eat) + -었- (past tense) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: ate
notes: 먹다 (meokda) is the verb 'to eat.' -었- (-eot-) is the past tense suffix. -어 (-eo) is a common informal (반말, banmal) ending for verbs and adjectives, often used in casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 오늘도
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 오늘 (today) + -도 (also, too)
meaning: today too, even today
notes: 오늘 (oneul) means 'today.' -도 (-do) is a particle meaning 'also,' 'too,' or 'even.' It attaches to nouns or pronouns. This usage is casual and informal.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 원 없이 V-다
meaning: To do something to one's heart's content, without any regret or limitation. Literally 'without desire/lack.'
example: 원 없이 놀았어. (I played to my heart's content.)
level: Intermediate

=== [06:34] 간만에. ===
meaning: For the first time in a while.
tone: Casual, brief, reflective.
--- word ---
term: 간만에
type: Noun + particle (colloquial contraction)
breakdown: 간만 (a long time) + -에 (at, in)
meaning: for the first time in a while, after a long time
notes: '간만' (ganman) literally means 'a long interval' or 'a long time.' -에 (-e) is a locative particle indicating time or place. '간만에' (ganman-e) is a common colloquial expression meaning 'for the first time in a while' or 'after a long time.' It is often used interchangeably with '오랜만에' (oraenman-e), which has the same meaning but is slightly more standard. Both are informal.

=== [07:56] 한자의 여유야. ===
meaning: It's the leisure of a single character.
tone: Casual, slightly cryptic or playful.
--- word ---
term: 한자의
type: Noun + possessive particle
breakdown: 한자 (Chinese character / one character) + -의 (possessive particle)
meaning: of a single character / of a Chinese character
notes: '한자' (hanja) typically refers to Chinese characters, but can also mean 'one character.' -의 (-ui) is the possessive particle, equivalent to 's' or 'of.' This phrase '한자의 여유' is unusual in this context. A very common and similar-sounding phrase is '한 잔의 여유' (han jan-ui yeoyu), meaning 'the leisure of a single drink/cup,' which would fit the relaxing context better. It's possible this is a mishearing in the transcript or a specific inside joke. This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 여유야
type: Noun + informal copula
breakdown: 여유 (leisure,余裕) + -야 (informal copula)
meaning: it's leisure
notes: '여유' (yeoyu) means 'leisure,' '余裕,' 'room,' or 'time to spare.' -야 (-ya) is the informal (반말, banmal) form of the copula '이다' (ida, to be), used when the preceding noun ends in a vowel. If it ended in a consonant, it would be '-이야' (-iya). This usage is casual and informal.

=== [08:16] 오늘도 하다 왔어? 잘했어. ===
meaning: You came after doing it today too? Good job.
tone: Casual, encouraging, appreciative.
--- word ---
term: 오늘도
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 오늘 (today) + -도 (also, too)
meaning: today too, even today
notes: 오늘 (oneul) means 'today.' -도 (-do) is a particle meaning 'also,' 'too,' or 'even.' It attaches to nouns or pronouns. This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 하다
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 (to do)
meaning: doing (as part of a compound action)
notes: '하다' (hada) means 'to do.' In the construction 'V-다 오다' or 'V-고 오다,' it implies completing an action before coming. Here, '하다 왔어' is likely a colloquial shortening of '하고 왔어' (hago wasseo), meaning 'did [something] and came (here).' The specific action is implied from context, likely a daily routine or fan activity. This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 왔어
type: Verb + past tense + informal ending
breakdown: 오다 (to come) + -았- (past tense) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: came
notes: 오다 (oda) is the verb 'to come.' -았- (-at-) is the past tense suffix. -어 (-eo) is a common informal (반말, banmal) ending for verbs and adjectives. The combined '하다 왔어?' implies 'Did you come after doing [something]?' or 'Did you do [something] and then come (here)?' This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 잘했어
type: Adverb + verb + past tense + informal ending
breakdown: 잘 (well) + 하다 (to do) + -았- (past tense) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: Good job. / You did well.
notes: '잘' (jal) is an adverb meaning 'well.' '하다' (hada) is the verb 'to do,' so '잘하다' (jalhada) means 'to do well.' -았- (-at-) is the past tense suffix, and -어 (-eo) is a common informal (반말, banmal) ending. '잘했어' (jalhaesseo) is a common informal expression meaning 'Good job!' or 'You did well!' This usage is casual and informal.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 오다
meaning: To do something and then come (back). Often implies completing an action before arriving at the current location.
example: 밥 먹고 왔어. (I ate and came.)
level: Beginner

=== [08:38] 예전처럼 기복이 있진 않은 것 같아. ===
meaning: It seems like there isn't as much fluctuation as before.
tone: Casual, reflective, observational.
--- word ---
term: 예전처럼
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 예전 (the past, old times) + -처럼 (like, as)
meaning: like before, as in the past
notes: '예전' (yejeon) means 'the past' or 'old times.' -처럼 (-cheoreom) is a particle meaning 'like' or 'as.' It indicates similarity or comparison. This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 기복이
type: Noun + subject particle
breakdown: 기복 (fluctuation, ups and downs) + -이 (subject particle)
meaning: fluctuation (as the subject)
notes: '기복' (gibok) means 'fluctuation,' 'ups and downs,' or 'variations.' It can refer to mood, performance, or any varying state. -이 (-i) is a subject particle, used when the preceding noun ends in a consonant. This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 있진
type: Verb + negative particle + topic particle (contraction)
breakdown: 있다 (to exist, to have) + -지 않다 (to not do) + -은 (topic particle)
meaning: not existing / not having (with emphasis)
notes: '있다' (itda) means 'to exist' or 'to have.' '-지 않다' (-ji anta) is a common way to form negative sentences, meaning 'to not do/be.' '있지 않다' (itji anta) means 'to not exist/have.' '있진' (itjin) is a contraction of '있지는' (itjineun), where the topic particle -는 (-neun) is used for emphasis or contrast, often implying 'not *that* much' or 'not *really*.' This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 않은
type: Adjective/Verb + negative + adjectival ending
breakdown: 않다 (to not do/be) + -은 (adjectival ending)
meaning: not (modifying a noun, here '것')
notes: '않다' (anta) is the negative auxiliary verb, used with -지 to negate a verb or adjective. -은 (-eun) is an adjectival ending used to modify a following noun (here, '것'). This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun (dependent noun)
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing, fact
notes: '것' (geot) is a dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'fact.' It's often used to nominalize clauses. This usage is casual and informal.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: Adjective + informal ending
breakdown: 같다 (to be like, to seem) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: it seems, it looks like
notes: '같다' (gatda) is an adjective meaning 'to be like' or 'to seem.' -아 (-a) is a common informal (반말, banmal) ending for verbs and adjectives. The pattern 'V/Adj-는 것 같다' (-neun geot gatda) is a common grammar pattern meaning 'it seems that...' or 'I think that...' This usage is casual and informal.
--- grammar ---
pattern: N-처럼
meaning: Indicates similarity, meaning 'like N' or 'as N.'
example: 꿈처럼 아름다워. (It's beautiful like a dream.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/Adj-지 않다
meaning: A common way to negate verbs or adjectives, meaning 'to not V/Adj.'
example: 가지 않아. (I don't go.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/Adj-는 것 같다
meaning: Used to express a supposition or opinion, meaning 'it seems that V/Adj' or 'I think that V/Adj.'
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (It seems like it's raining.)
level: Intermediate

=== [08:46] 체력관리? 체력관리를 잘해야 되는 것 같아. ===
meaning: Physical health management? I think you have to manage your physical health well.
tone: Reflective, slightly questioning, informal advice.
--- word ---
term: 체력관리
type: noun
breakdown: 체력관리
meaning: physical health management
notes: A compound noun meaning 'physical strength management' or 'health management'. 체력 (chaeryeok) means 'physical strength' or 'stamina', and 관리 (gwanri) means 'management' or 'care'.
--- word ---
term: ?
type: punctuation
breakdown: 
meaning: question mark
notes: Indicates a question or a moment of reflection/hesitation.
--- word ---
term: 체력관리를
type: noun + object particle
breakdown: 체력관리 + 를
meaning: physical health management + object particle
notes: 를 (reul) is the object particle, indicating that '체력관리' is the direct object of the verb.
--- word ---
term: 잘해야
type: adverb + verb conjugation
breakdown: 잘하다 (to do well) + -아야 하다 (must/have to) -> 잘해야 하다 -> 잘해야
meaning: must do well / have to manage well
notes: 잘하다 (jalhada) means 'to do well'. The ending -아야 하다 (-aya hada) or -어야 하다 (-eoya hada) means 'must' or 'have to'. It indicates necessity or obligation. This is a common pattern for giving advice. The '하다' part is often omitted in casual speech when followed by other verbs or endings, as seen here with '되는'.
--- word ---
term: 되는
type: verb + adnominal ending
breakdown: 되다 (to become/be) + -는 (present adnominal ending)
meaning: becoming / that is
notes: 되다 (doeda) means 'to become', 'to be', or 'to work out'. Here, it connects the preceding clause '잘해야' to the dependent noun '것'. The -는 (neun) ending makes the verb modify the following noun, functioning like 'that is' or 'which is' in English. It's part of the '-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다' pattern.
--- word ---
term: 것
type: dependent noun
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing / fact
notes: 것 (geot) is a dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'fact'. It is frequently used after verbs or adjectives with adnominal endings to nominalize clauses, allowing them to function as nouns. Here, it's part of the '-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다' pattern.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: adjective conjugation
breakdown: 같다 (to be like/seem) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: seems like / I think
notes: 같다 (gatda) means 'to be like' or 'to seem'. The -아 (-a) ending makes it an informal, present tense declarative or interrogative form. When combined with '-(으)ㄴ/는 것', it forms the common grammar pattern '-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다' meaning 'it seems that...' or 'I think that...'. This expresses the speaker's opinion or conjecture.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아야/어야 하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, meaning 'must' or 'have to'.
example: 공부해야 해요. (Gongbuhaeya haeyo.) - I have to study.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: Used to express the speaker's opinion, conjecture, or observation, meaning 'it seems that...', 'I think that...', or 'it looks like...'.
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (Biga oneun geot gatayo.) - I think it's raining.
level: Intermediate

=== [08:56] 요새는 좀 버거웠는데 ===
meaning: Lately, it's been a bit overwhelming/tough, but...
tone: Reflective, slightly struggling, transitional.
--- word ---
term: 요새는
type: adverb + topic particle
breakdown: 요새 (lately) + -는
meaning: lately (as for)
notes: 요새 (yosae) means 'lately' or 'these days'. -는 (neun) is the topic particle, highlighting 'lately' as the subject of the current discussion or providing contrast.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 좀
meaning: a bit / a little
notes: 좀 (jom) is a common adverb used to soften requests or statements, or to indicate 'a little' or 'a bit'. It can imply a slight degree of something.
--- word ---
term: 버거웠는데
type: adjective conjugation + connective ending
breakdown: 버겁다 (to be overwhelming/tough) + -었 (past tense) + -는데 (connective ending)

notes: 버겁다 (beogeopda) means 'to be overwhelming', 'to be beyond one's capacity', or 'to be tough'. The -었 (-eot) indicates past tense. -는데 (-neunde) is a versatile connective ending that provides background information, sets the scene, or introduces a contrast or a reason for the following statement. Here, it sets up a transition to the next thought, implying 'it was tough, and because of that...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는데/은데/는데
meaning: A connective ending used to provide background information, introduce a new topic, express contrast, or set up a reason for the following clause. It can often be translated as 'but', 'so', 'and', or 'however'.
example: 날씨가 좋은데 집에 있어요. (Nalssiga joeunde jibe isseoyo.) - The weather is good, but I'm at home.

meaning: More than exercise, the most important thing is sleep.
tone: Emphatic, advisory, informal.

meaning: more than exercise
notes: 운동 (undong) means 'exercise' or 'sport'. -보다 (boda) is a comparative particle meaning 'than' or 'compared to'. It's used to make comparisons between two nouns or clauses.

term: 제일
type: adverb
breakdown: 제일
meaning: most / the most
notes: 제일 (jeil) is an adverb meaning 'the most' or 'first'. It is used to indicate the highest degree of an adjective or adverb. It can be interchanged with 가장 (gajang).

term: 중요한

meaning: important
notes: 중요하다 (junghyohada) means 'to be important'. The -ㄴ (-n) ending is the adnominal ending for adjectives ending in a vowel (or -은 for consonants), making the adjective modify the following noun. Here, it modifies '거' (contraction of 것).
--- word ---
term: 거는
type: dependent noun + topic particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 (thing) + -은 (topic particle) -> 거는
meaning: the thing (as for)
notes: 거 (geo) is a common colloquial contraction of 것 (geot), meaning 'thing' or 'fact'. -는 (neun) is the topic particle, indicating that 'the important thing' is the topic of the sentence. The full form would be '것은'.
--- word ---
term: 잠이야
type: noun + copula
breakdown: 잠 (sleep) + -이야 (informal copula)
meaning: it is sleep
notes: 잠 (jam) means 'sleep'. -이야 (-iya) is the informal (반말, banmal) form of the copula 이다 (ida), meaning 'to be'. It's used when the preceding noun ends in a consonant. If the noun ended in a vowel, it would be -야 (-ya).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -보다
meaning: A comparative particle meaning 'than' or 'compared to'. It is attached to the noun or pronoun that is being compared against.
example: 사과보다 배가 더 좋아요. (Sagwaboda baega deo johayo.) - Pears are better than apples.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는
meaning: An adnominal ending that turns a verb or adjective into a modifier for a following noun. -ㄴ/은 is for adjectives (past/present), -는 is for present tense verbs.
example: 예쁜 꽃 (yeppeun kkot) - pretty flower; 읽는 책 (ingneun chaek) - book being read
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -이야/야
meaning: The informal (반말) conjugation of the copula 이다 (ida), meaning 'to be'. -이야 is used after nouns ending in a consonant, and -야 after nouns ending in a vowel.
example: 이건 내 책이야. (Igeon nae chaegiya.) - This is my book.
level: Beginner

breakdown: 생각하다 (to think) + -지만 (but/although)
meaning: even though you think
notes: 생각하다 (saenggakhada) means 'to think'. The ending -지만 (-jiman) means 'but' or 'although', introducing a contrasting idea. Here, it sets up the contrast between the fans' perception of their youth and the reality of shared aging.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ 거라고 생각하다
meaning: This pattern means 'to think that (someone) will be/do' or 'to think that (something) is likely to happen'. It uses the future/conjecture noun modifier -(으)ㄹ 거 followed by the quotative particle -라고 and the verb 생각하다 (to think).
example: 내일 비가 올 거라고 생각해요. (I think it will rain tomorrow.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -지만
meaning: The ending -지만 (-jiman) connects two clauses, meaning 'but' or 'although'. It introduces a contrasting or opposing idea.
example: 이 음식은 맛있지만 너무 매워요. (This food is delicious, but it's too spicy.)
level: Beginner

term: 이제

pattern: 그러니까
meaning: The conjunction 그러니까 (geureonikka) means 'so', 'therefore', or 'that's why'. It introduces a conclusion or result based on the preceding statement.

--- grammar ---
pattern: ~이다 (informal)
meaning: The informal copula -이야 (-iya) or -야 (-ya) means 'to be'. It attaches directly to a noun to state what something is. -이야 is used after nouns ending in a consonant, and -야 after nouns ending in a vowel.
example: 이것은 책이야. (This is a book.) / 이것은 사과야. (This is an apple.)

pattern: ~때
meaning: The noun ~때 (~ttae) means 'time' or 'when'. It is used to indicate a specific period or moment when an action or state occurs.
example: 어렸을 때 (when I was young) / 점심때 (at lunchtime)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~이/가 들다
meaning: When used with abstract nouns like 생각 (thought) or 마음 (heart/mind), 들다 (deulda) means 'to have' or 'to feel' a certain thought or emotion.
example: 좋은 생각이 들었어요. (I had a good idea.)

--- grammar ---
pattern: -거든
meaning: The ending -거든 (-geodeun) is used to provide background information or a reason for the preceding statement, often with a nuance of 'because, as you know' or 'if you're wondering why'. It's informal and conversational.

=== [10:20] 빨리 나이 먹고 싶다라는 ===
meaning: The thought of wanting to get older quickly.
tone: Reflective, slightly wistful, informal.
--- word ---
term: 빨리
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: quickly
notes: A common adverb meaning 'quickly' or 'fast'. Often used with verbs of motion or change. This is an informal register.
--- word ---
term: 나이
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: age
notes: Refers to one's age. In Korean, 'to get older' is commonly expressed as 나이 먹다 (nai meokda), literally 'to eat age'.
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: verb conjugation
breakdown: 먹다 (to eat/take) + -고 (and/then)
meaning: getting/taking
notes: The verb 먹다 (meokda) literally means 'to eat', but when combined with 나이 (nai, age), it means 'to get older' or 'to age'. The -고 (go) ending connects verbs, often implying sequence or continuation, but here it's part of the -고 싶다 (go sipda) desire pattern.
--- word ---
term: 싶다라는
type: verb ending + particle
breakdown: 싶다 (want to) + -라는 (that which is called/thought)
meaning: wanting to (that)
notes: 싶다 (sipda) is a verb meaning 'to want to' when attached to another verb stem (e.g., 먹고 싶다, 'want to eat'). -라는 (raneun) is a contraction of -라고 하는 (rago haneun), meaning 'saying that' or 'the thing called'. It introduces a reported thought or idea, functioning similarly to 'that' in English when introducing a thought. This is an informal register.

meaning: my
notes: A contraction of 나의 (naui), meaning 'my'. It is commonly used in informal contexts. The full form 나의 is also correct but slightly more formal or emphatic.

meaning: 30s (age group)
notes: Refers to people in their thirties. The suffix -대 (dae) is used to denote an age decade (e.g., 10대 for teens, 20대 for twenties). This is an informal register.
--- word ---
term: 의
type: particle
breakdown: 
meaning: of (possessive)
notes: A possessive particle, similar to 's or 'of' in English. In casual spoken Korean, it is often omitted, especially with common nouns or pronouns like 내.
--- word ---
term: 모습인
type: noun + copula
breakdown: 모습 (appearance/form) + 이다 (to be) + -ㄴ (adnominal ending)
meaning: being the appearance/form
notes: 모습 (moseup) refers to one's appearance, figure, or the way something looks or is. It can also mean 'state' or 'aspect'. 이다 (ida, to be) is the copula, and -ㄴ (n) is an adnominal ending that turns the preceding clause into a modifier for the following noun (것 in this case).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: dependent noun
breakdown: 
meaning: thing, fact
notes: A dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'fact'. It is frequently used in grammatical constructions, such as -ㄴ/은 것 같다 (n/eun geot gatda) to express conjecture.
--- word ---
term: 같아.
type: adjective conjugation
breakdown: 같다 (to be like/seem) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: it seems like, I think
notes: The base form 같다 (gatda) means 'to be like' or 'to seem'. When combined with an adnominalized clause (e.g., -ㄴ/은 것), it forms the pattern -ㄴ/은 것 같다, meaning 'it seems like' or 'I think'. The final -아 (a) makes it an informal (반말, banmal) ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -ㄴ/은 것 같다
meaning: Used to express conjecture or opinion, meaning 'it seems like', 'it looks like', or 'I think'. It attaches to verb stems (present: -는 것 같다, past: -ㄴ/은 것 같다, future: -ㄹ/을 것 같다) and adjective stems/copula (present: -ㄴ/은 것 같다).
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (It seems like it's raining.)
level: Intermediate

=== [11:31] 20대 청이들 어딨어? ===

breakdown: 20대
meaning: those in their 20s

term: 어딨어
type: Interrogative adverb + verb + informal ending (contraction)

The -어 (-eo) ending makes it a casual, informal (반말 - banmal) question. This is used when speaking to close friends, family, or, in this context, affectionately to fans.

=== [11:56] 오, 20대 많은데? ===
meaning: Oh, there are a lot of people in their 20s?
tone: Surprised, informal, questioning, interactive.
--- word ---
term: 오
type: interjection
breakdown: 오
meaning: Oh
notes: An interjection expressing surprise, realization, or mild exclamation. Similar to 'Oh' in English.
--- word ---
term: 20대
type: noun
breakdown: 20대
meaning: people in their 20s; the 20s age group
notes: The suffix -대 (-dae) is attached to numbers to refer to an age decade (e.g., 10대 for teens, 20대 for twenties, 30대 for thirties). It's a common way to categorize people by age group in Korean culture.
--- word ---
term: 많은데?
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 많다 (manta) + -은데 (-eunde)
meaning: there are many/a lot, but/and (implying a question)
notes: 많다 (manta) means 'to be many' or 'to be a lot'. The ending -은데 (-eunde) (or -는데 (-neunde) for verbs) is used to provide background information, express a mild contrast, or, as here, to pose a question with a nuance of surprise or seeking confirmation. It's an informal, conversational ending. The question mark indicates an interrogative tone.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는데 (-(eu)n/neunde)
meaning: This ending is versatile. It can introduce background information, express a mild contrast ('but'), or, when used with a rising intonation, form a question implying surprise or seeking confirmation. For adjectives, it's -(으)ㄴ데; for verbs, it's -는데.
example: 비 오는데 우산 있어요? (Bi oneunde usan isseoyo?) - It's raining, do you have an umbrella? (background) / 예쁜데 비싸요. (Yeppeunde bissayo.) - It's pretty, but expensive. (contrast)
level: Intermediate

=== [12:06] 내가 이렇게 많은 소녀들을 거느리고 있었던 말이야? ===
meaning: So I was leading/having so many girls, you mean?
tone: Playful, surprised, rhetorical question, taking on a metaphorical role.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 (na) + -가 (-ga)
meaning: I (subject)
notes: 나 (na) is the informal first-person pronoun 'I'. -가 (-ga) is the subject particle, indicating that 'I' am the one performing the action. The combination 내가 (naega) is a common contraction of 나 + 가.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: adverb
breakdown: 이렇게
meaning: like this, so (much)
notes: An adverb meaning 'like this' or 'in this way'. It can also intensify the following adjective or verb, meaning 'so' or 'this much'.
--- word ---
term: 많은
type: adjective (adnominal form)
breakdown: 많다 (manta) + -은 (-eun)
meaning: many (modifying a noun)
notes: This is the adnominal (attributive) form of the adjective 많다 (manta, to be many/a lot). -은 (-eun) is attached to adjective stems ending in a consonant (or -ㄴ (-n) for stems ending in a vowel) to modify a following noun. Here, it modifies 소녀들 (sonyeodeul, girls).
--- word ---
term: 소녀들을
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 소녀 (sonyeo) + -들 (-deul) + -을 (-eul)
meaning: girls (object)
notes: 소녀 (sonyeo) means 'girl' or 'maiden'. -들 (-deul) is the plural marker. -을 (-eul) is the object particle, indicating that 'girls' are the direct object of the verb 거느리고 있었다 (geoneurigo isseotda, was leading/having).
--- word ---
term: 거느리고
type: verb (continuous form)
breakdown: 거느리다 (geoneurida) + -고 (-go)
meaning: leading, having under one's care (and...)
notes: 거느리다 (geoneurida) means 'to lead', 'to have under one's care', or 'to command'. The -고 (-go) here is part of the -고 있다 (-go itda) continuous tense construction, indicating an ongoing action. It can also connect clauses, meaning 'and' or 'then'.
--- word ---
term: 있었던
type: verb (past continuous adnominal)
breakdown: 있다 (itda) + -었- (-eot-) + -던 (-deon)
meaning: was (doing something) (modifying a noun, implying past continuous/recollection)
notes: 있다 (itda) means 'to be' or 'to exist'. -었- (-eot-) is the past tense marker. -던 (-deon) is an adnominal ending used to describe an action or state that was ongoing or repeatedly occurred in the past, often with a sense of recollection or discovery. Here, it combines with -고 있다 to form a past continuous adnominal form, 'was leading/having'.
--- word ---
term: 말이야?
type: ending
breakdown: 말 (mal) + -이야 (-iya)
meaning: you mean? / is that what you're saying?
notes: A colloquial interrogative ending, often used to confirm something or express surprise, similar to 'You mean...?' or 'Is that what you're saying?'. It's an informal contraction of 말하는 거야? (malhaneun geoya?, Are you saying that?). It's commonly used in casual conversation among close friends or when speaking to oneself.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고 있다 (-go itda)
meaning: This pattern indicates an ongoing action or a continuous state, similar to the '-ing' form in English. It is formed by attaching -고 있다 to the verb stem.
example: 밥을 먹고 있어요. (Babeul meokgo isseoyo.) - I am eating rice.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는 말이야? (-(eu)n/neun mariya?)
meaning: A colloquial interrogative ending used to confirm something, express surprise, or ask for clarification, often with a nuance of 'You mean...?' or 'Is that what you're saying?'. It's an informal contraction of -(으)ㄴ/는 것이야? or -(으)ㄴ/는 말하는 거야?.
example: 네가 그랬단 말이야? (Nega geuraetdan mariya?) - You mean you did that?
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/던 (-(eu)n/deon)
meaning: This adnominal ending is used to describe a noun based on a past action or state. -(으)ㄴ is for completed actions/states. -던 is for actions/states that were ongoing, repeated, or habitually occurred in the past, often with a nuance of recollection or discovery. Here, it's used with a continuous verb form.
example: 내가 읽던 책 (Naega ikdeon chaek) - The book I was reading / The book I used to read.
level: Intermediate

meaning: So the 10s are girls, and the 30s are aunties?

--- word ---

type: noun + particle
breakdown: 10대 (sipdae) + -는 (-neun)
meaning: as for the 10s (topic)
notes: 10대 (sipdae) refers to people in their teens. -는 (-neun) is the topic particle, indicating that 'the 10s' are the subject of discussion, often contrasting them with another topic (here, the 30s).
--- word ---
term: 소녀고
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 소녀 (sonyeo) + -고 (-go)
meaning: are girls, and...
notes: 소녀 (sonyeo) means 'girl' or 'maiden'. Hwasa uses this term for her younger female fans (20s, and here, 10s). The particle -고 (-go) connects two clauses, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. Here, it links '10s are girls' with '30s are aunties'.
--- word ---
term: 30대는
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 30대 (samsipdae) + -는 (-neun)
meaning: as for the 30s (topic)
notes: 30대 (samsipdae) refers to people in their thirties. -는 (-neun) is the topic particle, highlighting 'the 30s' as the next topic, in contrast to the 10s.
--- word ---
term: 이모야?
type: noun + ending
breakdown: 이모 (imo) + -이야 (-iya)
meaning: are aunties? (informal question)
notes: 이모 (imo) means 'maternal aunt'. Hwasa uses this term affectionately for her female fans in their 30s. -이야 (-iya) (or -야 (-ya) after a vowel) is the informal declarative/interrogative form of the copula 'to be'. With a rising intonation, it forms a question. This confirms Hwasa's playful age-based categorization of her fans.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고 (-go)
meaning: This particle connects two or more clauses or actions in a sequential or conjunctive manner, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It can also connect nouns or adjectives.
example: 저는 학생이고 동생은 회사원이에요. (Jeoneun haksaengigo dongsaengeun hoesawonieyo.) - I am a student, and my younger sibling is an office worker.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -이야/-야 (-iya/-ya)
meaning: This is the informal form of the copula '이다' (ida, to be). It is used after nouns to mean 'is/am/are'. -이야 is used after a noun ending in a consonant, and -야 is used after a noun ending in a vowel. With a rising intonation, it forms an informal question.
example: 이거 뭐야? (Igeo mwoya?) - What is this? / 내 친구야. (Nae chinguya.) - It's my friend.
level: Beginner

=== [14:30] 진짜? 진짜 계셔? ===

meaning: really, truly
notes: 진짜 (jinjja) is an adverb meaning 'really' or 'truly', emphasizing the following verb.

type: verb + ending

meaning: are (you) present? (informal)

=== [14:45] 자고 계셔. ===
meaning: They're sleeping.
tone: Informal, gentle speculation, slightly endearing.
--- word ---
term: 자고
type: verb + connective ending
breakdown: 자다 + -고
meaning: sleeping (and)
notes: 자다 (jada) means 'to sleep.' The ending -고 (-go) here connects to the following verb, forming the continuous action '-고 있다' (go itda), meaning 'to be -ing.' In this informal context, the '있다' is implied or contracted into '계셔'.
--- word ---
term: 계셔
type: honorific verb (contracted)
breakdown: 계시다 + -어
meaning: are (honorific, informal)
notes: 계시다 (gyesida) is the honorific form of 있다 (itda), meaning 'to be' or 'to exist.' It is used to show respect to the subject of the verb. -어 (-eo) is an informal, casual ending. The full, polite form would be 계세요 (gyeseyo) or 계십니다 (gyesimnida). The speaker is referring to older fans, hence the use of honorifics.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고 있다 / -고 계시다
meaning: Indicates an ongoing action or state, similar to 'to be -ing' in English. When used with 계시다, it adds an honorific layer.
example: 밥 먹고 있어요. (I am eating.) / 주무시고 계세요. (He/She is sleeping - honorific).
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: 계시다 (honorific)
meaning: The honorific form of 있다 (itda, to be/exist) and 자다 (jada, to sleep, when used as 주무시다). Used to show respect to the person being referred to.
example: 할머니께서 집에 계세요. (Grandma is at home.)
level: Beginner

=== [14:46] 자고 계실 수도 있겠다. ===
meaning: They might be sleeping.
tone: Informal, gentle conjecture, empathetic.
--- word ---
term: 자고
type: verb + connective ending
breakdown: 자다 + -고
meaning: sleeping (and)
notes: 자다 (jada) means 'to sleep.' The ending -고 (-go) here connects to the following verb, forming the continuous action '-고 있다' (go itda), meaning 'to be -ing.' See notes for segment 0.
--- word ---

type: honorific verb + future/conjecture modifier
breakdown: 계시다 + -ㄹ
meaning: might be (honorific)
notes: 계시다 (gyesida) is the honorific form of 있다 (itda). The suffix -ㄹ (-l) is a future tense or conjecture modifier, often used with '수도 있다' to express possibility. It modifies the following noun '수' (su).
--- word ---
term: 수도
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 수 + -도
meaning: possibility (also)
notes: 수 (su) means 'way' or 'possibility.' -도 (-do) is a particle meaning 'also' or 'even.' Together with the preceding verb ending -ㄹ, it forms the pattern '-ㄹ 수도 있다' (l sudo itda), meaning 'it is possible that... / might... / could...'
--- word ---
term: 있겠다
type: verb + conjecture ending
breakdown: 있다 + -겠- + -다
meaning: it might be / I suppose it is
notes: 있다 (itda) means 'to be' or 'to exist.' -겠- (get-) indicates conjecture or the speaker's assumption/will. -다 (-da) is a declarative ending. The combination '-ㄹ 수도 있겠다' expresses a strong possibility or a speaker's reasonable guess.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -ㄹ 수도 있다
meaning: Expresses possibility or likelihood, meaning 'might,' 'could,' or 'it is possible that...'.
example: 비가 올 수도 있어요. (It might rain.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -겠- (conjecture)
meaning: Indicates the speaker's conjecture, assumption, or strong guess about a situation. Often translates to 'I suppose,' 'I guess,' 'it seems,' or 'must be.'
example: 피곤하시겠어요. (You must be tired.)
level: Beginner

term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not

meaning: An exclamatory ending used to express a mild surprise, realization, or observation. It conveys a sense of 'I see that...' or 'It seems that...'.

meaning: won't be, but / I guess there won't be, but
notes: 없다 (eop-da) means 'to not exist' or 'to not have'. The ending -겠지만 (-get-ji-man) combines -겠- (get-, expressing conjecture/presumption) and -지만 (-ji-man, expressing 'but' or 'although'). So, it means 'I suppose/expect there won't be, but...'. This implies a concession or a lead-in to another thought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -겠지만
meaning: Used to express a conjecture or presumption, followed by a contrast or concession. It means 'I suppose/expect... but' or 'It probably is... but'.
example: 바쁘겠지만 도와줄게. (You're probably busy, but I'll help you.)
level: Intermediate

--- grammar ---

meaning: This ending expresses the speaker's intention or obligation, meaning 'I should/must do X.' It's a stronger form of -아/어야 하다/되다, indicating a personal decision or realization.
example: 이제 공부해야겠어. (I should study now.)

breakdown: 말하다 + -(으)니까
meaning: because I say it, when I say it
notes: 말하다 means 'to speak' or 'to say.' The -(으)니까 ending indicates reason or cause, often implying a subsequent action or realization. It can also mean 'when/as (one does something)' leading to a result. Commonly used in spoken, informal contexts.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 진짜
meaning: really, truly
notes: A common colloquial adverb meaning 'really' or 'truly.' Can also be used as an exclamation 'Really?!' or 'Seriously?!' Informal.
--- word ---
term: 갈
type: Verb modifier
breakdown: 가다 + -(으)ㄹ
meaning: to go, that will go
notes: 가다 means 'to go.' The -(으)ㄹ ending attaches to verbs to modify a following noun, indicating future tense, possibility, or a general characteristic. Here, it modifies 길 (road/way) to mean 'the road to go' or 'the road that needs to be gone.'
--- word ---
term: 길이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 길 + -이
meaning: road, way
notes: 길 means 'road,' 'path,' or 'way.' -이 is the subject particle, indicating that 길 is the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 멀다
type: Descriptive verb
breakdown: 멀다
meaning: to be far, distant
notes: 멀다 means 'to be far' or 'to be distant.' In this context, '갈 길이 멀다' is an idiom meaning 'there's a long way to go' or 'there's a long road ahead,' implying a lot of effort or time is still needed for something. This is a common expression. Informal, declarative.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)니까
meaning: Used to express a reason or cause for the following statement, or to indicate that a certain action leads to a result or realization. Often used in spoken Korean.
example: 비가 오니까 집에 가요. (Because it's raining, I'm going home.)
level: Beginner/Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ 길
meaning: A noun modified by a future/prospective verb form, often indicating a path, method, or possibility for an action. '갈 길이 멀다' is a fixed idiom.
example: 할 일이 많아요. (There are many things to do.)
level: Intermediate

=== [16:39] 우리가 60년 더 사랑할게. ===

term: 우리가
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 우리 + -가
meaning: We
notes: 우리 means 'we' or 'us.' -가 is the subject particle. In Korean, 우리 is often used even when referring to 'my' or 'our' in a collective sense (e.g., 우리 엄마 - 'my mom,' literally 'our mom'). Here, it refers to Hwasa and her fans. Informal.

term: 60년

term: 더

breakdown: 더

term: 사랑할게
type: Verb + ending
breakdown: 사랑하다 + -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: I/we will love (you/each other)
notes: 사랑하다 means 'to love.' The -(으)ㄹ게 ending expresses the speaker's intention or promise to do something, often for the benefit of or in consideration of the listener. It's a casual, friendly, and often affectionate way to make a promise or state an intention. Used with first-person subjects (I/we).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: Used to express the speaker's intention or promise to do something, often with consideration for the listener. It's a casual, friendly, and often affectionate way to make a promise or state an intention. Used with first-person subjects (I/we).
example: 내일 전화할게. (I'll call you tomorrow.)
level: Beginner/Intermediate

type: Proper Noun
breakdown: 청이

term: 님
type: Honorific suffix
breakdown: 님
meaning: Mr./Ms./Mx., esteemed
notes: 님 is a general honorific suffix attached to names, titles, or nouns to show respect. Here, it's used with '갓 청이' to respectfully address the 'god-tier Cheong-i' (80-year-old fans). It elevates the formality and respect shown to the audience members, even within a playful context.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 안
meaning: not
notes: 안 is a short-form negative adverb placed directly before a verb or descriptive verb to negate it.
--- word ---
term: 계시죠
type: Verb + ending
breakdown: 계시다 + -죠
meaning: are (honorific), aren't they?
notes: 계시다 is the honorific form of 있다 (to be, to exist, to have). It's used when the subject is someone older or of higher status. The ending -죠 (contraction of -지요) is a confirmatory ending, asking for agreement or confirming something the speaker believes to be true. It's often translated as 'right?' or 'isn't it?' It makes the question softer and more conversational.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 님
meaning: Attaches to names, titles, or nouns to show respect to the person or entity.
example: 선생님 (teacher + nim), 사장님 (boss + nim)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: 안 + Verb/Adjective
meaning: Negates the verb or adjective. Placed directly before the word it negates.
example: 안 먹어요. (I don't eat.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)시-
meaning: Attached to verb/adjective stems to show respect for the subject of the sentence.
example: 가시다 (to go, honorific)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -죠
meaning: Asks for confirmation or agreement, often implying the speaker already knows or suspects the answer. Contraction of -지요.
example: 맛있죠? (It's delicious, isn't it?)
level: Beginner/Intermediate

=== [16:50] 그다음은 뭐라고 해야 돼? ===
meaning: What should it be called next?
tone: Informal, pondering, slightly exasperated as the speaker struggles to find the right term.
--- word ---
term: 그다음은
type: adverb + topic particle
breakdown: 그다음 + -은
meaning: next / as for the next one
notes: 그다음 (geuda-eum) means 'next' or 'after that'. -은/는 (eun/neun) is a topic particle, marking '그다음' as the topic of the sentence. It implies 'as for what comes next...'.
--- word ---
term: 뭐라고
type: interrogative pronoun + quotative particle
breakdown: 뭐 + -라고
meaning: what / what to say
notes: 뭐 (mwo) means 'what'. -라고 (rago) is a quotative particle used with verbs like 하다 (hada, to say/do). Here, 뭐라고 하다 means 'to say what' or 'to be called what'. It's a common way to ask 'What do you call it?' or 'What should I say?'.
--- word ---
term: 해야
type: verb conjugation
breakdown: 하다 + -아야
meaning: should do / must do
notes: 하다 (hada) is the verb 'to do'. -아야/어야 하다/되다 (aya/eoya hada/doeda) is a grammar pattern indicating obligation or necessity, meaning 'must do' or 'should do'. Here, it's conjugated to 해야 (haeya).
--- word ---
term: 돼?
type: verb conjugation + question marker
breakdown: 되다 + -어?
meaning: become? / should it be?
notes: 되다 (doeda) means 'to become' or 'to be'. In this context, combined with -아야/어야 하다/되다, it means 'should be' or 'is it necessary to be'. The informal ending -어 (eo) combined with a rising intonation forms a casual question. The full pattern -아야/어야 돼? is a common way to ask 'Should I/we...?' or 'Does it have to be...?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -라고 하다
meaning: Used to quote someone's words or to ask/state what something is called. It can mean 'to say that...' or 'to be called...'.
example: 이걸 뭐라고 해요? (Igeol mworago haeyo?) - What do you call this?
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아야/어야 하다/되다
meaning: Expresses obligation, necessity, or 'must/should do'. 하다 is generally used when the subject has control, 되다 when it's a general necessity or consequence.
example: 공부해야 돼요. (Gongbuhaeya dwaeyo.) - I have to study.
level: Beginner

=== [16:52] 잠깐만. ===

tone: Informal, conversational, indicating a brief pause for thought.
--- word ---
term: 잠깐만.
type: adverb + particle
breakdown: 잠깐 + -만
meaning: just a moment / wait a second
notes: 잠깐 (jamkkan) means 'a moment' or 'a short while'. -만 (man) is a particle meaning 'only' or 'just'. Together, 잠깐만 is a very common colloquial expression used to ask someone to wait briefly or to indicate that the speaker needs a moment to think or do something.

notes: The term '90대' (gusipdae) refers to people in their 90s. The suffix '대' (dae) is attached to numbers to indicate a decade or an age range. For example, '10대' (sipdae) means 'teens' or 'people in their 10s', '20대' (isipdae) means 'people in their 20s'. In this context, Hwasa is humorously struggling to come up with a suitable honorific for her fans in their 90s, repeating the term as she thinks.

=== [18:38] 근데 멍청이들도 100살이 돼도 비슷해 이제 할 거야? ===
meaning: But even if you silly Cheong-is turn 100, will you still be similar, will you do this now?
tone: Playful, challenging, affectionate. Hwasa is teasing her fans about their loyalty.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: conjunction
breakdown: 근데
meaning: but; however; by the way
notes: An informal, conversational conjunction used to introduce a new topic or a contrasting idea. It's a shortened form of 그런데 (geureonde).
--- word ---
term: 멍청이들도
type: noun + particles
breakdown: 멍청이 + 들 + 도
meaning: even the silly Cheong-is / even you silly fans
notes: 멍청이 (meongcheong-i) literally means 'idiot' or 'fool,' but Hwasa uses it affectionately and playfully for her fans, 'Cheong-i.' 들 (deul) is a plural marker. 도 (do) is a particle meaning 'even' or 'also,' emphasizing that this applies to 'even' the Cheong-is.
--- word ---
term: 100살이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 100살 + 이
meaning: 100 years old (subject)
notes: 100살 (baeksal) means '100 years old.' 살 (sal) is a native Korean counter for age. 이 (i) is the subject particle, indicating '100 years old' as the subject of the clause.
--- word ---
term: 돼도
type: verb ending
breakdown: 되다 + -아/어도
meaning: even if (you) become
notes: 되다 (doeda) means 'to become.' The ending -아/어도 (a/eodo) is a conjunctive ending meaning 'even if,' 'even though,' or 'regardless of.' It expresses a concession or condition.
--- word ---
term: 비슷해
type: adjective
breakdown: 비슷하다
meaning: similar; alike
notes: 비슷하다 (biseuthada) means 'to be similar' or 'to be alike.' Here, it's used in its informal, declarative form, implying 'will you still be similar (to how you are now)?'
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: adverb
breakdown: 이제
meaning: now; from now on
notes: 이제 (ije) refers to 'now' or 'from this point onward,' often implying a change or a new phase. It can be contrasted with 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now' or 'at this very moment' without necessarily implying a change.
--- word ---
term: 할
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 하다 + -(으)ㄹ
meaning: will do; to do (future/prospective modifier)
notes: 하다 (hada) means 'to do.' -(으)ㄹ (eu)l is a future or prospective adnominal ending, modifying an implied noun (e.g., 'the thing you will do'). In this context, it's part of the future interrogative form -(으)ㄹ 거야?.
--- word ---
term: 거야?
type: noun + particle + verb ending (contraction)
breakdown: 것 + 이 + -야? (contraction of 것이야?)
meaning: will (you) do it? / is it (that you will do)?
notes: This is a common informal contraction of 것이야? (geosiya?), which is the informal interrogative form of 것 (geot, thing) + 이다 (ida, to be). It's frequently used to ask about future plans or intentions, forming the future tense pattern V-(으)ㄹ 거야? (will you V?).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어도
meaning: Used to express a concession, meaning 'even if,' 'even though,' or 'regardless of.' It indicates that the action or state in the preceding clause does not affect the outcome in the following clause.
example: 비가 와도 갈 거예요. (I will go even if it rains.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄹ 거야?
meaning: An informal way to ask about future plans or intentions. It translates to 'Will you V?' or 'Are you going to V?'. The formal equivalent is V-(으)ㄹ 거예요?
example: 내일 올 거야? (Will you come tomorrow?)
level: Beginner

meaning: coming up, appearing (descriptive)

=== [19:31] 올라오지도 않아 ===
meaning: They don't even come up.
tone: Casual, slightly resigned or emphatic, confirming the previous rhetorical question.

term: 올라오지도
type: verb + ending + particle
breakdown: 올라오다 + -지 + 도
meaning: don't even come up
notes: 올라오다 (ollaoda) means 'to come up' or 'to appear.' -지 (ji) is a connective ending expressing confirmation or a rhetorical question. 도 (do) means 'even' or 'also'; when used with a negative verb, it intensifies the negation, meaning 'not even.'

breakdown: 않다 + -아
meaning: not (do)

--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -지 않다
meaning: The long-form negation for verbs and adjectives, meaning 'not to do [verb]' or 'not to be [adjective].'
example: 먹지 않아 (I don't eat).
level: Beginner

=== [19:40] 안 보여줘도 알지 어떤 상태에 있지? ===

tone: Casual, playful, rhetorical, assuming shared understanding with the audience about an unpresentable situation.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 안
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is a short-form negative adverb placed directly before a verb or adjective to negate it.
--- word ---
term: 보여줘도
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 보이다 + -어주다 + -어도
meaning: even if I show (you)
notes: 보이다 (boida) means 'to be seen' or 'to show.' -어주다 (eojuda) is an auxiliary verb construction meaning 'to do (something) for someone,' so 보여주다 means 'to show (for you).' -어도 (eodo) is a conjunctive ending meaning 'even if' or 'even though,' expressing concession.
--- word ---
term: 알지
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 알다 + -지
meaning: you know, right?
notes: 알다 (alda) means 'to know.' -지 (ji) is a common informal ending used to confirm something the speaker believes the listener already knows or agrees with, often translated as 'right?' or 'you know?'
--- word ---
term: 어떤
type: determiner/adjective
breakdown: 어떤
meaning: what kind of, which
notes: 어떤 (eotteon) is a determiner meaning 'what kind of,' 'which,' or 'some.' It precedes a noun to ask about its nature or type.
--- word ---
term: 상태에
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 상태 + -에
meaning: in (what) state/condition
notes: 상태 (sangtae) means 'state,' 'condition,' or 'status.' -에 (e) is a locative particle indicating location ('in,' 'at,' 'on') or the state in which something exists.
--- word ---
term: 있지?
type: verb + ending (interrogative)
breakdown: 있다 + -지?
meaning: it is, right? / is it in?
notes: 있다 (itda) means 'to be' or 'to exist.' -지? (ji?) is an informal interrogative ending used to confirm something, similar to 알지, but here it's part of a direct question about the state.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -아/어주다
meaning: Means 'to do (something) for someone.' It indicates a favor or an action performed for the benefit of another.
example: 도와주세요 (Please help me).
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -아/어도
meaning: Means 'even if,' 'even though,' or 'regardless of.' It expresses a concession.
example: 바빠도 괜찮아 (Even if I'm busy, it's okay).
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -지?
meaning: Used to confirm something the speaker believes the listener already knows or agrees with, often translated as 'right?' or 'you know?'
example: 맛있지? (It's delicious, right?)
level: Beginner

=== [21:24] 얘는 안 보여줄게 ===
meaning: I won't show you this one.
tone: Casual, playful, slightly secretive, referring to a specific item Hwasa doesn't want to reveal.

term: 얘는
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 얘 + -는
meaning: this one (as for)
notes: 얘 (yae) is a colloquial contraction of 이 아이 (i ai), meaning 'this child' or 'this person.' It can also be used informally to refer to an object, similar to 'this one' or 'it.' -는 (neun) is a topic particle, marking 얘 as the topic and often implying contrast ('as for this one...').
--- word ---
term: 안

meaning: not

meaning: I will show (you) / I won't show (you)
notes: 보이다 (boida) means 'to be seen' or 'to show.' -어주다 (eojuda) is an auxiliary verb construction meaning 'to do (something) for someone.' -ㄹ게 (eulge) is a volitional ending used when the speaker expresses their intention or promise to do something, often for the listener's benefit or with their implied agreement. It's a casual, personal promise.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -아/어주다
meaning: Means 'to do (something) for someone.' It indicates a favor or an action performed for the benefit of another.
example: 도와주세요 (Please help me).
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -ㄹ게/-을게
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or promise to do something, often for the listener's benefit or with their implied agreement.
example: 내가 전화할게 (I'll call you).
level: Beginner

=== [21:29] 근데 오늘 되게 막 비 오고 무대 젖어있고 ===
meaning: But today, it really rained a lot, and the stage was wet, and...
tone: Casual, descriptive, transitional, setting the scene for a performance story. The '막' adds a sense of intensity or spontaneity.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: conjunction
breakdown: 그런데
meaning: but, however, by the way
notes: 근데 (geunde) is a common colloquial contraction of 그런데 (geureonde). It functions as a conjunction meaning 'but,' 'however,' or 'by the way,' often used to introduce a new topic or a contrasting idea.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: noun/adverb
breakdown: 오늘
meaning: today
notes: 오늘 (oneul) means 'today.' It can function as both a noun and an adverb.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: adverb
breakdown: 되게
meaning: very, really
notes: 되게 (doege) is a colloquial adverb meaning 'very' or 'really,' intensifying the following adjective or verb.
--- word ---
term: 막
type: adverb
breakdown: 막
meaning: just, randomly, intensely
notes: 막 (mak) is a versatile colloquial adverb. Here, it conveys a sense of intensity or spontaneity, like 'it was really raining hard' or 'it just kept raining.'
--- word ---
term: 비
type: noun
breakdown: 비
meaning: rain
notes: 비 (bi) means 'rain.' When combined with 오다 (oda), 비가 오다 means 'to rain.'
--- word ---
term: 오고
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 오다 + -고
meaning: came, and
notes: 오다 (oda) means 'to come.' -고 (go) is a conjunctive ending that connects two or more actions or states in chronological order or simply lists them, translated as 'and' or 'and then.'
--- word ---
term: 무대
type: noun
breakdown: 무대
meaning: stage
notes: 무대 (mudae) means 'stage' (as in a performance stage), aligning with Hwasa's role as a performer mentioned in the STORY BIBLE.
--- word ---
term: 젖어있고
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 젖다 + -어 있다 + -고
meaning: was wet, and
notes: 젖다 (jeotda) means 'to get wet.' -어 있다 (eo itda) is a grammatical pattern indicating a state of being resulting from a completed action, so 젖어 있다 means 'to be wet' (having gotten wet). -고 (go) is a conjunctive ending meaning 'and.'
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -고
meaning: Connects two or more actions or states, often in chronological order or simply listing them. Can be translated as 'and' or 'and then.'
example: 밥 먹고 영화 봤어 (I ate and watched a movie).
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -아/어 있다
meaning: Indicates a state of being that is the result of a completed action. Often used with verbs that describe a change of state (e.g., 앉다 to 앉아 있다 - to be sitting).
example: 문이 열려 있어요 (The door is open).
level: Intermediate

=== [21:43] 너무 행복했어 ===
meaning: I was so happy.
tone: Casual, reflective, expressing strong positive emotion. This contrasts with the potentially negative description of the weather/stage in the previous sentence.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 너무
meaning: too, very, so
notes: 너무 (neomu) traditionally means 'too much' or 'excessively' (often with negative connotations). However, in modern colloquial Korean, it's very commonly used to simply mean 'very' or 'so' with positive adjectives/verbs, as seen here. This reflects Hwasa's positive attitude despite the rainy stage.
--- word ---
term: 행복했어
type: adjective (past tense)
breakdown: 행복하다 + -았/었 + -어
meaning: was happy
notes: 행복하다 (haengbokhada) means 'to be happy.' -았/었 (at/eot) is the past tense suffix, and -어 (eo) is an informal declarative ending. The STORY BIBLE notes Hwasa enjoys 'unexpected stage situations,' and this statement, following the description of a rainy, wet stage, exemplifies that enjoyment.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective + -았/었어
meaning: Past tense informal declarative ending for adjectives.
example: 예뻤어 (It was pretty).
level: Beginner

=== [21:44] 예상치도 못한 상황들이 좋더라 ===
meaning: I actually like unexpected situations.
tone: Casual, reflective, slightly enthusiastic, expressing a personal preference.
--- word ---
term: 예상치도
type: verb + particle
breakdown: 예상하다 + -지 않다 + -도
meaning: even unexpected
notes: This is a contraction of '예상하지도' (yesanghajido). '예상하다' means 'to expect'. '-지 않다' is a negative conjugation meaning 'not to do something'. '-도' is a particle meaning 'even' or 'also'. So, '예상하지도 못한' means 'even situations that I didn't expect'.
--- word ---
term: 못한
type: verb + adnominal ending
breakdown: 못하다 + -ㄴ
meaning: unable to do; that couldn't be done
notes: '못하다' (mothada) means 'to be unable to do' or 'to not do well'. Here, it functions as an adjective modifying '상황' (sanghwang, situation), meaning 'situations that couldn't be expected' or 'unexpected situations'. The '-ㄴ' is an adnominal ending for verbs, turning them into modifiers for nouns.
--- word ---
term: 상황들이
type: noun + plural marker + subject particle
breakdown: 상황 + -들 + -이
meaning: situations
notes: '상황' (sanghwang) means 'situation'. '-들' (deul) is a plural marker. '-이' (i) is a subject particle, indicating that '상황들' is the subject of the sentence. This is a casual, spoken form.
--- word ---
term: 좋더라
type: adjective + retrospective ending
breakdown: 좋다 + -더라
meaning: I found it good; it turned out to be good
notes: '좋다' (jota) means 'to be good'. The ending '-더라' (deora) is a retrospective ending used to express a past observation or realization. It conveys that the speaker experienced something and is now recalling or commenting on it. It's often used in informal speech, reflecting a personal discovery or feeling. For example, '맛있더라' (masitdeora) means 'It was delicious (I found out/realized it was)'. This ending is commonly used when sharing personal experiences or observations with others.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-지 않다
meaning: Used to express negation, meaning 'not to do/be something'.
example: 가지 않아요. (I'm not going.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-더라
meaning: A retrospective ending used to recall a past observation or realization. It conveys that the speaker personally experienced something and is now reporting or commenting on it. It implies a sense of discovery or a personal feeling about a past event.
example: 그 영화 정말 슬프더라. (That movie was really sad, I found.)
level: Intermediate

=== [21:50] 더 이렇게 뭐랄까 ===
meaning: It's more like, how should I put it...
tone: Casual, hesitant, searching for words, typical of spontaneous speech.
--- word ---
term: 더
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: more
notes: '더' (deo) is an adverb meaning 'more' or 'further'. It's used to indicate an increase in degree or quantity.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: like this; in this way
notes: '이렇게' (ireoke) is an adverb derived from '이렇다' (ireota, to be like this). It means 'like this' or 'in this way' and is often used to describe the manner of an action or state. It can also be used as a filler word when the speaker is thinking.
--- word ---
term: 뭐랄까
type: interrogative + verb ending (contraction)
breakdown: 뭐라고 할까
meaning: how should I put it; what should I say
notes: This is a common contraction of '뭐라고 할까' (mworago halkka). '뭐' (mwo) means 'what', '-라고' (rago) is a quotative particle, and '할까' (halkka) is 'should I do/say'. It's a colloquial expression used when the speaker is searching for the right words or trying to explain something in a specific way. It implies a slight hesitation or a request for understanding from the listener. It's informal.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 뭐라고 할까 (뭐랄까)
meaning: A colloquial expression used when the speaker is searching for the right words or trying to articulate a thought, similar to 'how should I put it?' or 'what should I say?'.
example: 이 맛은 뭐라고 할까... 독특해. (How should I describe this taste... it's unique.)
level: Intermediate

=== [21:54] 무대 하는 거에 있어서 긴장감이 더 이렇게 딱 생기고 ===
meaning: When it comes to performing on stage, the tension just builds up more like this, and...
tone: Casual, descriptive, slightly energetic, explaining a feeling.
--- word ---
term: 무대
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: stage
notes: '무대' (mudae) means 'stage'. In the context of Hwasa, an idol, it specifically refers to a performance stage.
--- word ---
term: 하는
type: verb + adnominal ending
breakdown: 하다 + -는
meaning: doing; performing
notes: '하다' (hada) means 'to do'. The '-는' (neun) is a present tense adnominal ending, turning the verb into a modifier for a noun (in this case, '것' implied before '거에'). So, '무대 하는 것' means 'the act of performing on stage'.
--- word ---
term: 거에
type: noun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 + -에
meaning: to/at the thing; in the case of
notes: This is a contraction of '것에' (geose). '것' (geot) is a dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'fact'. '-에' (e) is a particle indicating location, time, or in this context, 'when it comes to' or 'regarding'. 'V-는 거에 있어서' is a common pattern.
--- word ---
term: 있어서
type: verb + conjunctive ending
breakdown: 있다 + -어서
meaning: when it comes to; in terms of
notes: '있다' (itta) means 'to exist' or 'to be'. The '-어서' (eoseo) ending here functions to indicate a condition or a context, meaning 'when it comes to' or 'in terms of'. The pattern 'N에 있어서' or 'V-는 거에 있어서' is common for setting the scope of a statement. It's slightly more formal than just 'N에 대해서' (N-e daehaeseo, about N) but used casually here.
--- word ---
term: 긴장감이
type: noun + subject particle
breakdown: 긴장감 + -이
meaning: the feeling of tension; tension
notes: '긴장감' (ginjanggam) means 'a feeling of tension' or 'tension'. It's a compound of '긴장' (ginjang, tension) and '감' (gam, feeling). '-이' (i) is the subject particle.
--- word ---
term: 더
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: more
notes: As in segment 1, '더' (deo) means 'more'.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: like this; in this way
notes: As in segment 1, '이렇게' (ireoke) means 'like this' or 'in this way'. Here, it emphasizes how the tension builds up.
--- word ---
term: 딱
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: exactly; just; suddenly (with a sense of precision or firmness)
notes: '딱' (ttak) is a mimetic word or adverb that conveys a sense of precision, suddenness, or firmness. It can mean 'exactly', 'just', 'perfectly', or 'with a snap/click'. Here, it emphasizes the sudden and clear onset of the tension. It's a very common colloquial adverb.
--- word ---
term: 생기고
type: verb + conjunctive ending
breakdown: 생기다 + -고
meaning: arises and; forms and
notes: '생기다' (saenggida) means 'to arise', 'to form', or 'to appear'. The '-고' (go) ending connects clauses, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It indicates a sequence of events or a listing of facts.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는 거에 있어서
meaning: Used to specify the context or area to which a statement applies, meaning 'when it comes to V-ing' or 'in terms of V-ing'.
example: 공부하는 거에 있어서는 제가 좀 게을러요. (When it comes to studying, I'm a bit lazy.)
level: Intermediate

=== [22:03] 그래서 막 심장이 뛰더라고 ===

meaning: so; therefore
notes: '그래서' (geuraeseo) is a common conjunction meaning 'so' or 'therefore', indicating a result or consequence of the preceding statement. It's derived from '그러하다' (geureohada, to be so) + '-어서' (eoseo, because).
--- word ---
term: 막
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just; intensely; wildly; without restraint
notes: '막' (mak) is a versatile colloquial adverb. It can mean 'just', 'randomly', 'intensely', 'wildly', or 'without restraint'. Here, it emphasizes the intense and uncontrollable nature of her heart pounding, conveying excitement. It's a very common filler/intensifier in informal speech.

breakdown: 심장 + -이
meaning: heart
notes: '심장' (simjang) means 'heart'. '-이' (i) is the subject particle, indicating '심장' is the subject of '뛰더라고'.

breakdown: 뛰다 + -더라고
meaning: it was pounding (I realized/observed)

The '-더라고' (deorago) ending is a retrospective ending, similar to '-더라' (deora) in segment 0, but often implies a slightly more direct observation or a realization that occurred at the time. It conveys that the speaker experienced her heart pounding and is now recalling that experience. It's informal and expressive.

=== [22:05] 너무 즐거워서 ===

meaning: Used to indicate the reason or cause for the following clause, meaning 'because A/V' or 'so A/V'. It can also indicate a sequence of actions.
