=== [22:07] 행사 무대할 땐 어느 계절이 제일 좋냐고? ===
meaning: You're asking which season is best when performing at events?
tone: Casual, inquisitive, directly addressing the audience, likely reading a fan's question.
--- word ---
term: 행사
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: event
notes: '행사' (haengsa) means 'event', 'function', or 'occasion'. In the Kpop context, '행사 무대' (haengsa mudae) refers to performances at various events, often outside of a concert tour, like festivals or corporate events.
--- word ---
term: 무대할
type: noun + verb + adnominal ending
breakdown: 무대 + 하다 + -ㄹ
meaning: to perform on stage
notes: '무대하다' (mudaehada) is a verb phrase meaning 'to perform on stage'. Here, '무대할' is '무대하다' with the future/prospective adnominal ending '-ㄹ' (eul/l), modifying '때' (ttae, when). So, '무대할 때' means 'when performing on stage'.
--- word ---
term: 땐
type: dependent noun + topic particle (contraction)
breakdown: 때 + -는
meaning: when (as for)
notes: This is a contraction of '때는' (ttaeneun). '때' (ttae) means 'time' or 'when'. '-는' (neun) is the topic particle, highlighting 'when' as the topic of the question. 'V-ㄹ 때' means 'when V-ing'.
--- word ---
term: 어느
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: which
notes: '어느' (eoneu) is a determiner meaning 'which'. It's used before a noun to ask about a choice from a group.
--- word ---
term: 계절이
type: noun + subject particle
breakdown: 계절 + -이
meaning: season
notes: '계절' (gyejeol) means 'season'. '-이' (i) is the subject particle.
--- word ---
term: 제일
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: most; best
notes: '제일' (jeil) is an adverb meaning 'most' or 'best'. It's used with adjectives or adverbs to form the superlative degree (e.g., '제일 좋다' - best, '제일 빠르다' - fastest).
--- word ---
term: 좋냐고?
type: adjective + indirect question ending
breakdown: 좋다 + -냐고
meaning: are you asking if it's good?; asking which is best
notes: '좋다' (jota) means 'to be good'. The ending '-냐고' (nyago) is an informal indirect question ending. It's used when the speaker is quoting or referring to a question that was asked (or implied to be asked by the audience). Hwasa is likely reading a fan's question and repeating it back to confirm or acknowledge it. This is a common way for streamers to interact with chat comments.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-ㄹ 때
meaning: Used to indicate the time or occasion when an action occurs, meaning 'when V-ing'.
example: 밥 먹을 때 조용히 하세요. (Please be quiet when eating.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V-냐고 (묻다/하다)
meaning: An informal indirect question ending, used to report or refer to a question. Often used when the speaker is repeating a question they heard or saw (e.g., in a chat). The verb '묻다' (mutda, to ask) or '하다' (hada, to say) is often omitted in casual speech.
example: 뭐 하냐고? (Are you asking what I'm doing?)
level: Intermediate

=== [22:16] 개인적으로 난 겨울을 추운 데서 무대하는 걸 별로 안 좋아해 ===
meaning: Personally, I don't really like performing on stage in cold places in winter.
tone: Casual, expressing a personal preference.
--- word ---
term: 개인적으로
type: adverb
breakdown: 개인적 (gaeinjeok, personal) + -으로 (euro, adverbial particle)
meaning: personally
notes: The particle -으로 (-euro) is often attached to nouns or adjectival nouns to turn them into adverbs, meaning 'in a ... manner' or 'personally/individually' in this case. It can also mean 'by means of' or 'to/towards'.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: pronoun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 나 (na, I) + 는 (neun, topic particle)
meaning: I (as for me)
notes: This is a common contraction of 나 (na, I/me) and the topic particle -는 (-neun). It's used in casual speech. The full form is 나는 (naneun). 나 is the informal first-person pronoun.
--- word ---
term: 겨울을
type: noun + object particle
breakdown: 겨울 (gyeoul, winter) + -을 (eul, object particle)
meaning: winter (as object)
notes: The object particle -을/를 (-eul/reul) marks the direct object of a verb. -을 is used after a consonant-ending noun, and -를 after a vowel-ending noun.
--- word ---
term: 추운
type: descriptive verb (adnominal form)
breakdown: 춥다 (chupda, to be cold) + -은 (eun, adnominal ending)
meaning: cold
notes: This is the adnominal form of the descriptive verb 춥다 (chupda, to be cold). The -은 (-eun) ending is used for descriptive verbs ending in a consonant (except 'ㅂ' irregulars like 춥다, which drops 'ㅂ' and adds '우' before the ending). It modifies the following noun, meaning 'a cold [noun]' or '[noun] that is cold'.
--- word ---
term: 데서
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 데 (de, place/spot) + -서 (seo, at/from)
meaning: at a place
notes: 데 (de) here refers to a 'place' or 'spot'. The particle -서 (-seo) indicates the location where an action takes place, similar to 'at' or 'in'. It can also indicate 'from' or 'because'.
--- word ---
term: 무대하는
type: noun + verb + adnominal form
breakdown: 무대 (mudae, stage) + 하다 (hada, to do) + -는 (neun, adnominal ending)
meaning: performing on stage
notes: 무대하다 (mudaehada) means 'to perform on stage'. The -는 (-neun) ending is used for action verbs in the present tense to modify a following noun, meaning 'the [noun] that is doing [verb]'. Here, it modifies the implied '것' (geot, thing/act) in '걸'.
--- word ---
term: 걸
type: noun + object particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 (geot, thing/fact) + -을 (eul, object particle)
meaning: the act of doing something (as object)
notes: This is a common contraction of 것 (geot, thing/fact/act) and the object particle -을 (-eul). It's used in casual speech. The full form is 것을 (geoseul). It often nominalizes a preceding verb phrase, turning it into a noun phrase that can be the object of a sentence.
--- word ---
term: 별로
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not particularly, not really
notes: 별로 (byeollo) is an adverb that almost always appears with a negative verb or adjective. It means 'not particularly', 'not really', or 'not much'. For example, 별로 안 좋아해 (byeollo an joahae) means 'I don't particularly like it'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb (negative)
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is a short-form negative adverb placed directly before the verb or adjective it negates. It's equivalent to -지 않다 (-ji anta). For example, 안 좋아해 (an joahae) means 'don't like'.
--- word ---
term: 좋아해
type: verb (informal polite)
breakdown: 좋아하다 (joahada, to like) + -아/어 (a/eo, informal polite ending)
meaning: like
notes: This is the informal polite (해요-style) conjugation of 좋아하다 (joahada, to like). It's commonly used in everyday conversations, especially with friends, family, or fans in a casual setting like a livestream.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는 것
meaning: Nominalizes a verb phrase, turning it into a noun phrase meaning 'the act of doing V' or 'the fact that V'.
example: 책 읽는 것을 좋아해요. (I like reading books.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는 데서
meaning: Indicates 'at a place where V happens' or 'in a situation where V happens'.
example: 사람 많은 데서 이야기하는 걸 싫어해요. (I dislike talking in crowded places.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: 별로 + negative
meaning: Means 'not particularly', 'not really', or 'not much' when used with a negative verb or adjective.
example: 별로 안 매워요. (It's not particularly spicy.)
level: Beginner

=== [22:32] 그때는 이제 나는 동굴 속으로 들어가서 겨울잠을 자는 스타일이고 ===
meaning: At that time, I'm the type to go into a cave and hibernate.
tone: Casual, descriptive, slightly humorous.
--- word ---
term: 그때는
type: adverb + topic particle
breakdown: 그때 (geuttae, at that time) + -는 (neun, topic particle)
meaning: at that time (as for then)
notes: 그때 (geuttae) means 'at that time' or 'then'. The topic particle -는 (-neun) highlights 'that time' as the topic of the sentence, often implying a contrast or specific focus.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, from now on
notes: 이제 (ije) means 'now' or 'from now on', often implying a change in state or a new phase. It's different from 지금 (jigeum), which simply means 'right now' or 'at this moment'.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: pronoun + topic particle
breakdown: 나 (na, I) + -는 (neun, topic particle)
meaning: I (as for me)
notes: 나 (na) is the informal first-person pronoun. -는 (-neun) is the topic particle, indicating that 'I' is the subject being discussed.
--- word ---
term: 동굴
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: cave
notes: 동굴 (donggul) refers to a natural cave. Hwasa uses this metaphorically to describe her desire to retreat and stay indoors during winter.
--- word ---
term: 속으로
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 속 (sok, inside) + -으로 (euro, to/into)
meaning: into the inside
notes: 속 (sok) means 'inside' or 'interior'. The particle -으로 (-euro) indicates direction ('to', 'into') or means ('by way of'). Here, it means 'into the inside of' or 'into'.
--- word ---
term: 들어가서
type: verb (connective)
breakdown: 들어가다 (deureogada, to enter) + -아서/어서 (aseo/eoseo, and then/because)
meaning: enter and then
notes: The -아서/어서 (-aseo/eoseo) ending connects two clauses, indicating either a sequential action ('and then') or a reason/cause ('because'). Here, it signifies a sequence of actions: entering and then hibernating.
--- word ---
term: 겨울잠을
type: noun + object particle
breakdown: 겨울잠 (gyeouljam, hibernation) + -을 (eul, object particle)
meaning: hibernation (as object)
notes: 겨울잠 (gyeouljam) literally means 'winter sleep', referring to hibernation. The object particle -을 (-eul) marks it as the direct object of the verb 자다 (jada, to sleep).
--- word ---
term: 자는
type: verb (adnominal form)
breakdown: 자다 (jada, to sleep) + -는 (neun, adnominal ending)
meaning: sleeping
notes: This is the present tense adnominal form of 자다 (jada, to sleep). The -는 (-neun) ending modifies the following noun (스타일, style), meaning 'a style that sleeps' or 'the sleeping style'.
--- word ---
term: 스타일이고
type: noun + connective ending
breakdown: 스타일 (seutail, style) + -이고 (igo, and/is and)
meaning: is a style and
notes: 스타일 (seutail) is a loanword meaning 'style'. The ending -이고 (-igo) connects two clauses, meaning 'is [noun] and' or 'and is [noun]'. It can also imply that the following clause provides additional information or a consequence.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는 스타일이다
meaning: Means 'to be the type of person who does V' or 'to be the style of doing V'.
example: 나는 일찍 일어나는 스타일이에요. (I'm the type to wake up early.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아서/어서
meaning: Connects two clauses, indicating a sequence of actions ('and then') or a reason/cause ('because').
example: 밥을 먹어서 배불러요. (I ate and so I'm full. / I'm full because I ate.)
level: Beginner

=== [22:36] 습하지 않은 여름과 봄 ===
meaning: Summers and springs that aren't humid.
tone: Casual, descriptive, listing preferences.
--- word ---
term: 습하지
type: descriptive verb (negative stem)
breakdown: 습하다 (seupada, to be humid) + -지 (ji, negative particle)
meaning: not humid
notes: This is the stem of the descriptive verb 습하다 (seupada, to be humid) followed by -지 (-ji), which is used to form negative sentences with 않다 (anta) or 말다 (malda). Here, it's followed by 않은 (aneun) from 않다.
--- word ---
term: 않은
type: auxiliary verb (adnominal form)
breakdown: 않다 (anta, not to be/do) + -은 (eun, adnominal ending)
meaning: not (doing/being)
notes: 않다 (anta) is an auxiliary verb used to form negative expressions (V-지 않다). The -은 (-eun) ending makes it an adnominal (modifying) form, meaning 'that is not [humid]'.
--- word ---
term: 여름과
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 여름 (yeoreum, summer) + -과 (gwa, and)
meaning: summer and
notes: 여름 (yeoreum) means 'summer'. The particle -과/와 (-gwa/wa) means 'and' or 'with'. -과 is used after a consonant-ending noun, and -와 after a vowel-ending noun.
--- word ---
term: 봄
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: spring
notes: 봄 (bom) means 'spring'. It's one of the four seasons.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/Adj-지 않다
meaning: A common way to form negative sentences, meaning 'not to do V' or 'not to be Adj'.
example: 저는 커피를 마시지 않아요. (I don't drink coffee.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/Adj-지 않은 N
meaning: Modifies a noun (N) to mean 'N that does not V' or 'N that is not Adj'.
example: 재미있지 않은 영화. (A movie that is not interesting.)
level: Intermediate

=== [23:07] 뭐랄까 ===
meaning: How should I put it?
tone: Casual, reflective, seeking the right words.
--- word ---
term: 뭐랄까
type: interrogative + verb ending (colloquial contraction)
breakdown: 뭐 (mwo, what) + -라고 할까 (rago halkka, shall I say)
meaning: what should I say, how should I put it
notes: This is a common colloquial contraction of 뭐라고 할까 (mworago halkka), which literally means 'what shall I say?' or 'what should I call it?'. It's used when the speaker is searching for the right word or expression, similar to 'how should I put it?' or 'what's the word?'. It's very natural in casual conversation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-ㄹ/을까?
meaning: Asks 'shall I V?' or 'should I V?', often expressing a suggestion, a question about one's own action, or wondering about something.
example: 점심 뭐 먹을까? (What shall we eat for lunch?)
level: Beginner

--- grammar ---

meaning: Used to recall a past personal experience or observation. It implies the speaker is sharing something they realized or discovered at that time.

--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다

=== [23:50] 홍콩 3월에 갔을 때 ===

type: verb (past tense modifier form)

meaning: went (when)
notes: This is the past tense adnominal form of 가다 (gada, 'to go'). The -았/었- (at/eot) indicates past tense, and -(으)ㄹ (eul) is a future/conjectural adnominal ending, which here combines with 때 (ttae) to mean 'when (something happened in the past)'.
--- word ---
term: 때
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: time, when
notes: A noun meaning 'time' or 'moment'. When preceded by a verb in the -(으)ㄹ form, it creates the grammar pattern 'when (doing/did something)'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Used to indicate 'when' an action occurs or occurred. The verb before it is in its future/conjectural adnominal form. For past events, the past tense marker -았/었- is added before -(으)ㄹ.
example: 밥 먹을 때 (when eating), 한국에 갔을 때 (when I went to Korea)

meaning: Ma-sa-hoe (as for)
notes: 마사회 (Ma-sa-hoe) refers to a specific event or venue, possibly related to the Korea Racing Authority, where Hwasa performed. The STORY BIBLE notes it should be kept as 'Ma-sa-hoe'. -는 (neun) is the topic particle, here used to introduce new information about 'Ma-sa-hoe' or to contrast it with the previous topic (Hong Kong).
--- word ---
term: 4월이고
type: noun + connective ending
breakdown: 4월 + -이- + -고
meaning: is April and
notes: 4월 (sa-wol) means 'April'. -이- (i-) is the copula 'to be' (이다) conjugated, and -고 (go) is a connective ending meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It links two clauses, often implying a sequence or simply listing facts. Here, it links 'Ma-sa-hoe was in April' to the next thought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(이)고
meaning: A connective ending that links two or more clauses. It can mean 'and', 'and then', or 'and also'. When attached to a noun, the copula 이다 (ida, 'to be') is used, becoming -이고 (igo) after a consonant or -고 (go) after a vowel (though for nouns, it's usually -이고).
example: 저는 학생이고 제 친구는 선생님이에요. (I am a student, and my friend is a teacher.)
level: Beginner

=== [23:54] 맞나? ===
meaning: Is that right?
tone: Informal, questioning, seeking confirmation.
--- word ---
term: 맞나?
type: verb (informal interrogative ending)
breakdown: 맞다 + -나
meaning: Is it right?
notes: 맞다 (matda) means 'to be correct' or 'to be right'. -나 (na) is an informal interrogative ending, often used when speaking to oneself or to close friends/juniors, seeking confirmation or expressing a slight doubt. It's a banmal ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -나?
meaning: An informal interrogative ending used to ask a question, often to oneself or to someone close. It can express wonder, doubt, or a simple check for confirmation. It's a banmal form.
example: 벌써 왔나? (Did they already come?)
level: Intermediate

=== [24:02] 그 전달에 간 홍콩 ===
meaning: Hong Kong, which I went to the month before that.
tone: Informal, clarifying, descriptive.
--- word ---
term: 그
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: that
notes: A demonstrative determiner meaning 'that'. It refers to something previously mentioned or understood in context.
--- word ---
term: 전달에
type: noun + time particle
breakdown: 전달 + -에
meaning: in the month before
notes: 전달 (jeondal) means 'the previous month' or 'the month before'. -에 (e) is the time particle 'in'.
--- word ---
term: 간
type: verb (past tense modifier form)
breakdown: 가다 + -ㄴ
meaning: went
notes: This is the past tense adnominal form of 가다 (gada, 'to go'). The -ㄴ (n) ending attaches to verbs to modify a following noun, indicating a completed action. Here, it modifies 홍콩 (Hongkong), meaning 'Hong Kong that I went to'.
--- word ---
term: 홍콩
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Hong Kong
notes: The name of the city. See notes for segment 1.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ
meaning: An adnominal ending attached to verbs to modify a following noun, indicating a completed action (past tense). It's used after verbs ending in a vowel (e.g., 가다 -> 간) or after verbs ending in ㄹ (e.g., 살다 -> 산). For verbs ending in a consonant (except ㄹ), -은 is used (e.g., 먹다 -> 먹은).
example: 내가 읽은 책 (the book I read), 어제 본 영화 (the movie I saw yesterday)
level: Beginner

=== [24:04] 3월이 근데? ===

--- word ---
term: 3월이
type: noun + subject particle
breakdown: 3월 + 이
meaning: March + subject particle
notes: 3월 (sam-wol) means 'March'. 이 (i) is the subject particle used when the preceding noun ends in a consonant. It marks the noun as the subject of the sentence. This is a Beginner level particle.
--- word ---
term: 근데?
type: conjunction + implied question
breakdown: 근데 + ?
meaning: But / By the way?
notes: 근데 (geun-de) is a common conversational conjunction meaning 'but' or 'by the way'. It's often used to introduce a new topic, a contrasting idea, or to ask a follow-up question. The question mark implies a rising intonation, making it a question. It's informal and frequently used in daily speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -이/가 (subject particle)
meaning: Marks the subject of a sentence. Used with nouns ending in a consonant (이) or a vowel (가).
example: 날씨가 좋아요. (The weather is good.)
level: Beginner

=== [24:06] 그치? ===
meaning: Right?
tone: Informal, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 그치?
type: interjection/question tag
breakdown: 그치 + ?
meaning: Right? / Isn't it?
notes: 그치 (geu-chi) is an informal contraction of 그렇지 (geu-reo-chi), which comes from 그렇다 (geu-reo-ta, to be so/that way). It's used as a question tag to seek agreement or confirmation, similar to 'right?' or 'isn't it?' in English. It's very common in casual conversation. The full form 그렇지요 (geu-reo-chi-yo) is more polite.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -지? (question tag)
meaning: Attached to a verb or adjective stem to ask for confirmation or agreement, often expecting an affirmative answer. Informal form.
example: 이거 맛있지? (This is delicious, right?)
level: Beginner

=== [24:13] 홍콩은 3월이야 ===
meaning: Hong Kong was in March.
tone: Informal, declarative, clarifying.
--- word ---
term: 홍콩은
type: noun + topic particle
breakdown: 홍콩 + 은
meaning: Hong Kong + topic particle
notes: 홍콩 (hong-kong) is 'Hong Kong'. 은 (eun) is the topic particle, used when the preceding noun ends in a consonant. It highlights 'Hong Kong' as the topic of the sentence, indicating that the speaker is talking about the Hong Kong trip/event.
--- word ---
term: 3월이야
type: noun + informal copula
breakdown: 3월 + 이야
meaning: March + is/was
notes: 3월 (sam-wol) is 'March'. 이야 (i-ya) is the informal form of the copula 이다 (i-da, to be), used after a noun ending in a consonant. It functions like 'is' or 'was' depending on context. The polite form is 이에요 (i-e-yo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -은/는 (topic particle)
meaning: Marks the topic of a sentence, often indicating what the sentence is about or contrasting it with something else. Used with nouns ending in a consonant (은) or a vowel (는).
example: 저는 학생이에요. (As for me, I am a student.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -이야/예요 (informal copula)
meaning: The informal conjugation of the verb 이다 (to be). Used after a noun to state its identity. 이야 is used after a consonant, 예요 after a vowel.
example: 이것은 책이야. (This is a book.)
level: Beginner

=== [24:15] 그때 모든 스태프들과 ===
meaning: At that time, with all the staff...
tone: Informal, descriptive, setting the scene.
--- word ---
term: 그때
type: adverbial noun
breakdown: 
meaning: At that time / Then
notes: 그때 (geu-ttae) refers to a specific point in the past, 'at that time' or 'then'. It functions as an adverbial phrase.
--- word ---
term: 모든
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: All / Every
notes: 모든 (mo-deun) is a determiner meaning 'all' or 'every'. It modifies the following noun, indicating completeness or universality.
--- word ---
term: 스태프들과

breakdown: 스태프 + 들 + 과
meaning: Staff + plural marker + with/and
notes: 스태프 (seu-tae-peu) means 'staff' (from English 'staff'). 들 (deul) is a plural marker, making it 'staff members'. 과 (gwa) is a particle meaning 'with' or 'and', used when the preceding noun ends in a consonant. The speaker is indicating that she was 'with all the staff'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -들 (plural marker)
meaning: Attached to nouns to indicate plurality. While often used, it's not always mandatory in Korean when plurality is clear from context.
example: 학생들 (students)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -과/와 (with/and particle)
meaning: Connects two nouns meaning 'and', or indicates accompaniment meaning 'with'. Used with nouns ending in a consonant (과) or a vowel (와).
example: 친구와 영화를 봤어요. (I watched a movie with a friend.)

breakdown: 
meaning: Just / Wildly / Without restraint

term: 홍콩

breakdown: 
meaning: Hong Kong
notes: 홍콩 (hong-kong) is 'Hong Kong'.

meaning: Just / Wildly / Without restraint

meaning: While going around / While frequenting

=== [24:30] 너무 행복해가지고 ===
meaning: Because I was so happy...
tone: Informal, expressive, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Too / Very
notes: 너무 (neo-mu) means 'too' or 'very'. It intensifies the degree of the following adjective or adverb. While traditionally meaning 'too much' (with a negative connotation), in modern colloquial Korean, it's often used simply to mean 'very' or 'so' in a positive sense, as it is here.
--- word ---
term: 행복해가지고
type: adjective + conjunctive ending
breakdown: 행복하다 + -아/어가지고
meaning: Because I was happy / So happy that...
notes: 행복하다 (haeng-bok-ha-da) means 'to be happy'. -아/어가지고 (-a/eo-ga-ji-go) is an informal conjunctive ending that expresses a reason or cause for the following action or state. It's a colloquial variant of -아서/어서 (a-seo/eo-seo) or -아/어서 그런지 (a-seo/eo-seo geu-reon-ji). The sentence trails off, implying that the happiness led to something else (e.g., good memories, enjoying the trip even more). This ending is very common in spoken Korean.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어가지고 (because, so)
meaning: An informal conjunctive ending indicating the reason or cause for the following clause. It's a colloquial variant of -아서/어서 and often implies a direct consequence or result. It's frequently used in spoken Korean.
example: 배고파가지고 밥 먹었어요. (Because I was hungry, I ate.)
level: Intermediate

=== [24:34] 진짜 1년치 행복을 느꼈다니까 ===
meaning: I really felt a year's worth of happiness, I tell you.
tone: Informal, emphatic, expressive, recounting a strong feeling.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: adverb
breakdown: 진짜
meaning: really, truly
notes: As in segment 0, used for emphasis. Its repetition here further stresses the intensity of the feeling.
--- word ---
term: 1년치
type: noun
breakdown: 1년 + -치
meaning: a year's worth
notes: 1년 (illyeon) means 'one year'. The suffix -치 (-chi) is attached to nouns, especially units of time or quantity, to mean 'worth of' or 'amount of'. E.g., 하루치 (haruchi, a day's worth), 한 달치 (handalchi, a month's worth).
--- word ---
term: 행복을
type: noun + object particle
breakdown: 행복 + -을
meaning: happiness (object)
notes: 행복 (haengbok) means 'happiness'. -을 (-eul) is the object particle, used when the preceding noun ends in a consonant. If it ended in a vowel, -를 (-reul) would be used.
--- word ---
term: 느꼈다니까
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 느끼다 (to feel) + -었- (past tense) + -다니까
meaning: felt, I tell you / because I said I felt
notes: 느끼다 (neukkida) is the verb 'to feel'. -었- (-eot-) is the past tense marker. -다니까 (-danikka) is an informal, emphatic ending used to state something as a fact, often to persuade or remind the listener, or to express surprise/frustration. It implies 'because I said (it was so)' or 'I tell you (it was so)'. It's a contraction of -다고 하니까 (-dago hanikka).
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~치
meaning: Indicates 'worth of' or 'amount of' a certain unit or quantity.
example: 하루치 일 (haruchi il) - a day's worth of work
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~다니까
meaning: An informal, emphatic ending used to state a fact, often with a nuance of 'I tell you' or 'because I said so'. It can also express mild surprise or frustration.
example: 나도 간다니까! (Nado gandannikka!) - I'm going too, I tell you!
level: Intermediate

=== [24:36] 아 이 힘으로면은 ===
meaning: Ah, with this strength/energy, then...
tone: Informal, contemplative, transitional, expressing a thought process.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: interjection
breakdown: 아
meaning: Ah, Oh
notes: A common interjection expressing realization, surprise, or a pause in thought.
--- word ---
term: 이
type: demonstrative adjective
breakdown: 이
meaning: this
notes: Used to refer to something close to the speaker. Corresponds to 그 (geu, that - near listener) and 저 (jeo, that - far from both).
--- word ---
term: 힘으로면은
type: noun + particle + conditional + topic particle
breakdown: 힘 (strength/energy) + -으로 (with/by means of) + -이면 (if it is) + -은 (topic particle)
meaning: if it's with this strength/energy, then...
notes: 힘 (him) means 'strength' or 'energy'. -으로 (-euro) is a particle indicating means, instrument, or direction. -으로면은 (-euromyeoneun) is a colloquial contraction of -으로 이면은 (-euro imyeoneun), which combines -으로 (by means of), 이다 (to be), -으면 (conditional 'if'), and -은/는 (topic particle). It implies 'if it is by means of this...' or 'if it's with this... then...'. The topic particle -은/는 (-eun/neun) emphasizes the condition.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~으로 (~(eu)ro)
meaning: Indicates the means, instrument, or material used to perform an action. Can also indicate direction or status.
example: 펜으로 쓰다 (peneuro sseuda) - to write with a pen
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~으로면은 (~(eu)romyeoneun)
meaning: A colloquial contraction meaning 'if it is with/by means of... then...'. It sets up a conditional statement based on the preceding means or instrument.
level: Intermediate

=== [24:38] 한 1년 견딜 수 있겠다 싶을 정도의 ===
meaning: To the extent that I thought I could endure for about a year.
tone: Informal, reflective, descriptive, expressing a degree of feeling.
--- word ---
term: 한
type: determiner
breakdown: 한
meaning: about, approximately
notes: Used before numbers or quantities to indicate an approximation. E.g., 한 시간 (han sigan, about an hour).
--- word ---
term: 1년
type: noun
breakdown: 1년
meaning: one year
notes: As in segment 1.
--- word ---
term: 견딜
type: verb (future/potential modifier)
breakdown: 견디다 (to endure) + -ㄹ
meaning: to endure (future/potential)
notes: 견디다 (gyeondida) means 'to endure' or 'to bear'. -ㄹ (-l) is a future/potential adnominal ending, modifying the following noun (수 in this case) to indicate ability or possibility.
--- word ---
term: 수
type: dependent noun
breakdown: 수
meaning: ability, way, possibility
notes: A dependent noun that always follows a verb in the ~ㄹ/을 form. It is typically followed by 있다 (itda, to have/exist) or 없다 (eopda, to not have/exist) to express ability or possibility.
--- word ---
term: 있겠다
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 있다 (to be/exist) + -겠- (conjecture) + -다 (declarative ending)
meaning: would be able to, I guess I could
notes: 있다 (itda) means 'to be' or 'to exist'. -겠- (-get-) is a versatile suffix that can indicate intention, possibility, or conjecture/guess. Here, it expresses a conjecture or a thought about a future possibility: 'I guess I would be able to' or 'I think I could'.
--- word ---
term: 싶을
type: verb (future/potential modifier)
breakdown: 싶다 (to want/think/feel) + -ㄹ
meaning: to think/feel (future/potential)
notes: 싶다 (sipda) means 'to want to' when following a verb, but here it follows a clause (견딜 수 있겠다) and means 'to think/feel that...'. -ㄹ (-l) is again a future/potential adnominal ending, modifying 정도 (jeongdo).
--- word ---
term: 정도의
type: noun + possessive particle
breakdown: 정도 (extent/degree) + -의
meaning: to the extent of
notes: 정도 (jeongdo) means 'extent', 'degree', or 'level'. -의 (-ui) is the possessive particle, equivalent to 'of' or 's in English. It connects 정도 to the preceding clause, indicating 'to the extent that...'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~ㄹ/을 수 있다
meaning: Expresses ability or possibility, meaning 'can' or 'to be able to'.
example: 한국말 할 수 있어요. (Hangungmal hal su isseoyo.) - I can speak Korean.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~겠다
meaning: Indicates conjecture, intention, or possibility. Often translated as 'will', 'would', 'I guess', or 'I think'.
example: 맛있겠다! (Masitgetda!) - That looks delicious! (I guess it will be delicious!)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~ㄹ/을 정도
meaning: Indicates 'to the extent that...' or 'to the degree that...'. It describes the level or intensity of an action or state.
example: 눈물이 날 정도로 슬펐어요. (Nunmuri nal jeongdoro seulpeosseoyo.) - I was so sad that tears came out.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~다 싶다
meaning: Used to express 'to think that...' or 'to feel that...' about a preceding statement or situation.
example: 그가 올 거다 싶었어요. (Geuga ol geoda sipeosseoyo.) - I thought he would come.
level: Intermediate

=== [24:40] 행복이었어 ===
meaning: It was happiness.
tone: Informal, concluding, definitive, summarizing the feeling.
--- word ---
term: 행복이었어
type: noun + past tense copula + informal ending
breakdown: 행복 (happiness) + -이었- (past tense of 이다) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: it was happiness
notes: 행복 (haengbok) means 'happiness'. -이었- (-ieot-) is the past tense form of the copula 이다 (ida, to be), used when the preceding noun ends in a consonant. -어 (-eo) is the informal, casual ending for declarative sentences. The full formal equivalent would be 행복이었습니다 (haengbogieosseumnida).
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~이었어
meaning: The informal past tense form of the copula 이다 (to be), meaning 'it was' or 'they were'. Used after nouns ending in a consonant. If the noun ends in a vowel, it's ~였어 (~yeosseo).

=== [24:43] 그냥 어디 가다가 이렇게 ===
meaning: Just, while going somewhere, like this...
tone: Informal, descriptive, setting the scene for a memory.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: adverb
breakdown: 그냥
meaning: just, simply, without any particular reason
notes: A very common adverb used to indicate something is done without a specific purpose or effort, or to simply state a fact. Can also mean 'as is' or 'still'.
--- word ---
term: 어디
type: adverb/pronoun
breakdown: 어디
meaning: somewhere, where
notes: Can function as an interrogative pronoun ('where?') or an indefinite pronoun/adverb ('somewhere', 'anywhere'). Here, it's 'somewhere'.
--- word ---
term: 가다가
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 가다 (to go) + -다가
meaning: while going, on the way
notes: 가다 (gada) means 'to go'. -다가 (-daga) is a connective ending indicating that an action was interrupted by another action, or that one action occurred during the course of another. It implies 'while doing X, Y happened' or 'doing X, and then Y'.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + -게
meaning: like this, in this way
notes: Derived from 이렇다 (ireota, to be like this). -게 (-ge) turns adjectives into adverbs. It describes the manner in which an action is performed. Corresponds to 그렇게 (geureoke, like that - near listener) and 저렇게 (jeoreoke, like that - far from both).
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~다가
meaning: Indicates that an action or state is interrupted by another, or that one action occurs during the course of another. It can imply a sequence of events or a change in action.
example: 밥 먹다가 전화가 왔어요. (Bap meokdaga jeonhwaga wasseoyo.) - While eating, a call came.
level: Beginner

=== [24:45] 길거리에 카페 막 ===
meaning: Cafes on the street, like...
tone: Informal, descriptive, trailing off as the speaker continues to visualize the scene.
--- word ---
term: 길거리에
type: noun + locative particle
breakdown: 길거리 (street) + -에
meaning: on the street
notes: 길거리 (gilgeori) means 'street' or 'roadside'. -에 (-e) is a locative particle indicating location ('at', 'on', 'in') or time. Here, it specifies the location of the cafes.
--- word ---
term: 카페
type: noun
breakdown: 카페
meaning: cafe
notes: A loanword from English 'cafe'.
--- word ---
term: 막
type: adverb
breakdown: 막
meaning: just, randomly, all over, impulsively
notes: A versatile colloquial adverb. Here, it suggests 'just' or 'all over the place', implying a casual, widespread presence of cafes. It can also mean 'just now' or 'recklessly'.

=== [24:46] 노천 카페 같은데 앉아가지고 ===
meaning: Sitting in an open-air cafe or something like that, and then...
tone: Informal, descriptive, continuing the narrative of a pleasant memory.
--- word ---
term: 노천
type: noun
breakdown: 노천
meaning: open-air, outdoor
notes: 노천 (nocheon) refers to something being 'open-air' or 'outdoors', often used for cafes, baths, or markets.
--- word ---
term: 카페
type: noun
breakdown: 카페
meaning: cafe
notes: As in segment 6.
--- word ---
term: 같은데
type: adjective + dependent noun (contracted)
breakdown: 같다 (to be like) + -은 (adnominal ending) + 데 (place/thing) [contraction of 곳/것]
meaning: like (a place/thing), such as
notes: 같다 (gatda) means 'to be like' or 'to be the same'. -은 (-eun) is an adnominal ending. 데 (de) is a dependent noun that can refer to a 'place', 'thing', or 'situation'. Here, 같은데 (gateunde) is a common contraction of 같은 곳 (gateun got, a place like) or 같은 것 (gateun geot, a thing like), meaning 'a place/thing like that' or 'such as'. It implies 'an open-air cafe or similar place'.
--- word ---
term: 앉아가지고
type: verb + connective ending
breakdown: 앉다 (to sit) + -아 가지고
meaning: sitting and then (as a result/sequence)
notes: 앉다 (antda) means 'to sit'. -아/어 가지고 (-a/eo gajigo) is a colloquial connective ending that indicates a sequence of actions where the first action is a prerequisite or cause for the second, or simply a close temporal sequence. It's similar to -고 (-go, and) but often implies a stronger cause-and-effect or a more direct flow. The full form is -아/어 가지고, but it's often shortened to -아/어 갖고 (-a/eo gakko) or just -아/어 (-a/eo) in very casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~같은데
meaning: A colloquial expression meaning 'like (a place/thing)' or 'such as'. It's a contraction of ~같은 곳/것 (~gateun got/geot).
example: 영화 같은데 가고 싶어. (Yeonghwa gateunde gago sipeo.) - I want to go to a place like a movie theater (or similar).
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~아/어 가지고
meaning: A colloquial connective ending indicating that the first action leads to or is followed by the second action, often implying a cause-and-effect or a close temporal sequence. It can be translated as 'doing X and then Y' or 'having done X, Y'.
example: 밥 먹어 가지고 배불러. (Bap meogeo gajigo baebulleo.) - I ate and now I'm full.
level: Intermediate

=== [24:49] 커피 마시고 하는데도 너무 행복하고 ===

tone: Informal, reflective, slightly trailing, expressing contentment.
--- word ---
term: 커피
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: coffee
notes: A loanword from English 'coffee'. Commonly used in everyday Korean.
--- word ---
term: 마시고
type: verb + connective ending
breakdown: 마시다 + -고
meaning: drink and
notes: 마시다 (masida) means 'to drink'. The -고 (-go) ending connects two or more actions or states in chronological order or as a list. It can mean 'and' or 'and then'. This is a casual form.
--- word ---
term: 하는데도
type: verb + connective ending + particle
breakdown: 하다 + -는데도
meaning: even though I do
notes: 하다 (hada) means 'to do'. -는데도 (-neundedo) is a combination of the background/contrastive ending -는데 (-neunde) and the particle -도 (-do, meaning 'even' or 'also'). It expresses 'even though' or 'despite doing/being'. This is an informal, conversational form.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: too, very, excessively
notes: Originally meant 'too much' or 'excessively' with a negative connotation, but in modern colloquial Korean, 특히 (teukhi) among younger generations, it's often used simply to mean 'very' or 'really' in both positive and negative contexts, as seen here with 'happy'.
--- word ---
term: 행복하고
type: adjective + connective ending
breakdown: 행복하다 + -고
meaning: happy and
notes: 행복하다 (haengbokhada) means 'to be happy'. The -고 (-go) ending connects this state to a following, unstated thought, implying continuation or listing.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고
meaning: Connects two or more actions or states, often implying chronological order or a simple listing ('and', 'and then').
example: 밥 먹고 영화 봤어요. (Bap meokgo yeonghwa bwasseoyo.) - I ate and then watched a movie.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는데도
meaning: Expresses 'even though' or 'despite' a certain situation or action. It highlights a contrast or unexpected outcome.
example: 피곤한데도 공부했어요. (Pigonhandedo gongbuhaesseoyo.) - Even though I was tired, I studied.
level: Intermediate

=== [24:53] 2월도 좋다고 그랬었던 거 같은데 ===
meaning: I think I said February was good too.
tone: Informal, recollecting, slightly uncertain or reflective.
--- word ---
term: 2월
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: February
notes: 2월 (iwol) is 'February'. Months are formed by combining the Sino-Korean number with 월 (wol) for 'month'.
--- word ---
term: 도
type: particle
breakdown: 
meaning: also, too
notes: The particle -도 (-do) adds the meaning of 'also', 'too', or 'even' to the preceding noun or pronoun. It can replace subject or object particles.
--- word ---
term: 좋다고
type: adjective + indirect quotation
breakdown: 좋다 + -다고
meaning: said it was good
notes: 좋다 (jota) means 'to be good'. -다고 (-dago) is the indirect quotation ending for adjectives, meaning 'that (someone said) it is/was...'. The verb 'to say' (하다) is implied here, as it often is in conversational Korean.
--- word ---
term: 그랬었던
type: verb + past perfect + adnominal ending
breakdown: 그렇다 + -았/었었- + -던
meaning: had said/done (recollection)
notes: 그렇다 (geureota) means 'to be so' or 'to do so'. -았/었었- (-at/eosseot-) is the past perfect tense, indicating an action completed in the past that is no longer relevant or has a distant past feel. -던 (-deon) is a retrospective adnominal ending used to recall an action or state that occurred repeatedly or continuously in the past, or to describe something that was in the past. The full form is 그랬었다고 (geuraesseotdago) + 하다 (hada) + -던.
--- word ---
term: 거
type: noun (contraction)
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing, fact
notes: 거 (geo) is a common colloquial contraction of 것 (geot), meaning 'thing', 'fact', or 'abstract concept'. It's frequently used in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 같은데
type: adjective + connective ending
breakdown: 같다 + -은데
meaning: it seems like, but
notes: 같다 (gatda) means 'to be like' or 'to seem'. -은데 (-eunde) is a connective ending that provides background information, expresses a mild contrast, or sets the stage for a following statement. Here, it implies 'it seems like that, but (I'm not entirely sure)' or 'it seems like that, so...'. Hwasa uses this to express a thought that is slightly uncertain or trailing.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -다고 하다
meaning: Used for indirect quotation of adjectives. It means 'to say that [adjective]'. For verbs, it's -ㄴ/는다고 하다.
example: 친구가 예쁘다고 했어요. (Chinguga yeppeudago haesseoyo.) - My friend said it was pretty.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -았/었었-
meaning: The past perfect tense, indicating an action or state that was completed in the more distant past, often with a sense of finality or that it's no longer the case. It emphasizes that the past event is completely over.
example: 어렸을 때 여기에 살았었어요. (Eoryeosseul ttae yeogie sarasseosseoyo.) - I used to live here when I was young (but not anymore).
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -던
meaning: A retrospective adnominal ending used to recall an action or state that occurred repeatedly, continuously, or was in progress in the past. It describes something that 'used to be' or 'was (doing)'.
example: 제가 읽던 책이에요. (Jega ikdeon chaegieyo.) - It's the book I was reading (or used to read).
level: Intermediate

meaning: really, truly

breakdown: 따뜻하다 + -고
meaning: warm and

meaning: A colloquial conjunction (contraction of 그런데) used to introduce a new sentence or clause. It can mean 'but', 'however', 'and', 'so', or 'by the way', depending on context. It often signals a shift in thought or a mild contrast.

breakdown: 습하다 + -더라
meaning: it was humid (I observed)

=== [25:26] 진짜 습하긴 하더라 ===

--- word ---

breakdown: 진짜
meaning: really, truly
notes: A very common colloquial adverb used to emphasize or intensify the following word or phrase. It can also be used as an exclamation meaning 'Really?!' or 'Seriously!'. It's frequently used in casual conversation.

breakdown: 습하다 + -기 + -는
meaning: being humid, indeed

breakdown: 하다 + -더라

It's often used when sharing a personal anecdote or observation with others, as Hwasa is doing here, recalling her experience with the humidity in Hong Kong (from STORY BIBLE context).
--- grammar ---

meaning: Used to emphasize a verb or adjective, often implying a concession or contrast ('it is true that X, but...').
example: 예쁘긴 예뻐요. (Yeppeugin yeppeoyo.) It is pretty, indeed (but...).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -더라
meaning: Used to recall a past observation or realization made by the speaker. It implies the speaker personally witnessed or experienced something and is now sharing that memory.
example: 그 영화 재미있더라. (Geu yeonghwa jaemiitdeora.) That movie was interesting (I recall).

=== [25:30] 왜 그렇게 놀라는 거야 ===
meaning: Why are you so surprised?
tone: Informal, direct question, slightly playful.
--- word ---
term: 왜
type: adverb
breakdown: 왜
meaning: why

type: adverb
breakdown: 그렇게
meaning: like that, so
notes: An adverb derived from 그렇다 (geureota, to be so/that way). It means 'in such a way,' 'so much,' or 'like that.' Here, it intensifies '놀라는' (getting surprised), meaning 'why are you getting *so* surprised?'
--- word ---

type: verb
breakdown: 놀라다 + -는
meaning: getting surprised, surprising

--- word ---
term: 거야
type: ending
breakdown: 것이다 + -야
meaning: it is (that), is it (that)
notes: A colloquial contraction of -는 것이다 (-neun geosida) or -을 것이다 (-eul geosida), often used in informal speech to ask for an explanation, express a strong opinion, or confirm something. In questions, it seeks clarification or reason. Here, Hwasa is asking her fans for the reason behind their surprise.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는 거야?
meaning: A colloquial question ending used to ask for an explanation or reason, often implying 'Is it that...?' or 'Why is it that...?'
example: 뭐 하는 거야? (Mwo haneun geoya?) What are you doing?
level: Beginner

=== [26:31] 이사 진짜 힘든 거 같아 ===

--- word ---
term: 거
type: noun (contraction)
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing, fact
notes: A colloquial contraction of the dependent noun 것 (geot, thing/fact). It's very common in spoken Korean and functions similarly to '것' but is less formal. Here, it refers to the 'act' or 'experience' of moving.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: adjective
breakdown: 같다 + -아
meaning: seems like, looks like
notes: This is the informal (반말, banmal) conjugation of the adjective 같다 (gatda, to be like/similar to). When preceded by a verb or adjective in its descriptive form (e.g., -ㄴ/은 것 같다, -는 것 같다), it expresses the speaker's conjecture or opinion, meaning 'it seems that...' or 'I think that...'. Hwasa is sharing her personal impression about moving.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -ㄴ/은/는 것 같다
meaning: Used to express the speaker's opinion, conjecture, or impression, meaning 'it seems that...', 'I think that...', or 'it looks like...'. The form depends on whether the preceding word is an adjective or verb, and its tense.
example: 비가 올 것 같아요. (Biga ol geot gatayo.) It seems like it will rain.
level: Beginner

meaning: Used to indicate a specific time or period when an action or state occurs, meaning 'when' or 'at the time of'.

--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective + -게
meaning: Transforms an adjective into an adverb, describing the manner in which an action is performed.
example: 천천히 말하세요. (Cheoncheonhi malhaseyo.) Please speak slowly.

--- grammar ---

meaning: The suffix -게 is attached to an adjective stem to turn it into an adverb, modifying a following verb. It means 'in a ... way' or 'to ...ly'.

meaning: and felt sluggish/heavy
notes: 찌뿌둥하다 (jjibbudunghada) is an adjective describing a feeling of being sluggish, heavy, or stiff, often after waking up or when feeling unwell. The -고 (go) ending connects it to the next implied clause, similar to 'and' or 'and also'. This is a common expression for feeling physically unwell or tired.
--- word ---
term: 항상
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: always
notes: See notes for segment 0.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-고
meaning: Connects two or more clauses, indicating a sequence of actions or states, or simply listing them. It can mean 'and', 'and then', or 'and also'.
example: 날씨가 맑고 바람이 불어요. (The weather is clear and the wind is blowing.)
level: Beginner

=== [27:44] 좀 그런 건 있었거든 ===
meaning: I always had something like that. / There was always something like that.
tone: Informal, reflective, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a bit, a little
notes: Colloquial abbreviation of 조금 (jogeum). Softens the statement. See notes for segment 0.
--- word ---
term: 그런
type: demonstrative adjective
breakdown: 그렇다 (geureota) + -ㄴ (n)
meaning: such, that kind of
notes: 그런 (geureon) is the adjectival form of 그렇다 (geureota, to be so/that way). It means 'such a' or 'that kind of'. It modifies the following noun. It's often used to refer back to something previously mentioned or understood from context.
--- word ---
term: 건
type: contraction (noun + particle)
breakdown: 것 (geot) + -은 (eun)
meaning: thing/fact (topic)
notes: 건 (geon) is a common contraction of 것 (geot, thing/fact) + -은 (eun, topic particle). The topic particle -은/는 (eun/neun) marks the topic of the sentence, often indicating contrast or introducing new information. Using the contracted form 건 is very common in casual spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 있었거든
type: verb ending
breakdown: 있다 (itta) + -었- (eot) + -거든 (geodeun)
meaning: there was (because/you know)
notes: 있다 (itta) means 'to exist' or 'to have'. -었- (eot) is the past tense marker. -거든 (geodeun) is an explanatory ending used to provide background information or a reason for a previous statement, often with a nuance of 'you know' or 'because'. It's commonly used in informal, conversational settings, especially when the speaker assumes the listener might not know the information.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-았/었거든
meaning: Used to provide a reason or background explanation for a previous statement, often implying 'because...' or 'you know, the thing is...'. It's informal and conversational.
example: 어제 너무 피곤했거든. (I was so tired yesterday, you know.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + -은/는 것 -> N + -ㄴ/는 건
meaning: Contraction of the nominalizer 것 (thing/fact) with the topic particle -은/는. Very common in spoken Korean.
example: 그것은 -> 그건 (That thing is...)
level: Beginner

=== [27:46] 그리고 자려고 누우면 이제 두통이랑 ===
meaning: And when I lay down to sleep, now with the headache and...
tone: Informal, descriptive, transitional.
--- word ---
term: 그리고
type: conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: and, then
notes: See notes for segment 1.
--- word ---
term: 자려고
type: verb ending
breakdown: 자다 (jada) + -려고 (ryeogo)
meaning: in order to sleep
notes: 자다 (jada) means 'to sleep'. -려고 (ryeogo) is a grammatical ending attached to verbs to express intention or purpose, meaning 'in order to' or 'to intend to'. It is used when the subject of the main clause and the subordinate clause is the same.
--- word ---
term: 누우면
type: verb ending
breakdown: 눕다 (nupda) + -으면 (eumyeon)
meaning: if/when I lie down
notes: 눕다 (nupda) means 'to lie down'. It's an irregular verb (ㅂ-irregular). When followed by a vowel, ㅂ changes to ㅜ (u) or ㅗ (o). Here, 눕다 + -으면 becomes 누우면. -으면 (eumyeon) is a conditional ending meaning 'if' or 'when'. It describes a condition or a habitual action that occurs when a certain condition is met.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now (from this point on)
notes: 이제 (ije) means 'now', specifically referring to 'from this point on' or 'at this new stage'. It often implies a change in situation or a new beginning. This contrasts with 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now' or 'at this very moment' without necessarily implying a change.
--- word ---
term: 두통이랑
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 두통 (dutong) + -이랑 (irang)
meaning: with the headache / headache and
notes: 두통 (dutong) means 'headache'. -이랑 (irang) is a particle meaning 'with' or 'and' (listing items). It's the informal equivalent of -와/과 (wa/gwa). It is used after nouns ending in a consonant. In this context, it implies 'along with the headache'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-려고
meaning: Expresses intention or purpose, meaning 'in order to' or 'to intend to'. The subject of both clauses must be the same.
example: 밥을 먹으려고 식당에 갔어요. (I went to the restaurant to eat.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-으면/면
meaning: Expresses a condition ('if') or a habitual action ('when').
example: 비가 오면 집에 있을 거예요. (If it rains, I'll stay home.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + -이랑/랑
meaning: Connects nouns meaning 'and' or 'with'. It's the colloquial form of -와/과.
example: 친구랑 영화를 봤어요. (I watched a movie with a friend.)
level: Beginner

=== [27:50] 몸에 열이 너무 나서 ===

term: 몸에

breakdown: 몸 (mom) + -에 (e)

breakdown: 열 (yeol) + -이 (i)
meaning: fever/heat (subject)
notes: 열 (yeol) means 'heat' or 'fever'. -이 (i) is the subject particle, indicating that 'heat' is the subject of the verb 나다 (nada, to come out/occur).
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: too much, excessively
notes: An adverb meaning 'too much' or 'excessively'. It can be used with both positive and negative connotations, but often implies an undesirable excess. In casual speech, it's also used to mean 'very' or 'really' (e.g., 너무 좋다, 'it's so good').
--- word ---
term: 나서
type: verb ending
breakdown: 나다 (nada) + -아서 (aseo)
meaning: because it came out/occurred
notes: 나다 (nada) means 'to come out', 'to occur', or 'to break out' (e.g., 열이 나다, 'to have a fever'). -아서/어서 (aseo/eoseo) is a connective ending indicating cause or reason, meaning 'because' or 'so'. The preceding clause is the reason for the following clause.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-아서/어서
meaning: Connects two clauses, where the first clause is the reason or cause for the second clause. It means 'because' or 'so'.
example: 배고파서 밥을 먹었어요. (Because I was hungry, I ate.)
level: Beginner

=== [27:54] 이번 집은 잠을 별로 못 잤는데 ===
meaning: In this house, I couldn't sleep much, but...
tone: Informal, contrasting, descriptive.
--- word ---
term: 이번
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: this time, this
notes: 이번 (ibeon) means 'this time' or 'this (current)'. It functions as a determiner modifying the following noun, '집' (house), to mean 'this current house'.
--- word ---
term: 집은
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 집 (jip) + -은 (eun)
meaning: house (topic)
notes: 집 (jip) means 'house'. -은 (eun) is the topic particle, used after nouns ending in a consonant. Here, it introduces 'this house' as the topic, often implying a contrast with a previous house (as per the Story Bible, she moved).
--- word ---
term: 잠을
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 잠 (jam) + -을 (eul)
meaning: sleep (object)
notes: 잠 (jam) means 'sleep'. -을 (eul) is the object particle. See notes for segment 5.
--- word ---
term: 별로
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not particularly, not really
notes: 별로 (byeollo) is an adverb that must be used with a negative expression (e.g., not good, not much). It means 'not particularly' or 'not really'. If used alone, it implies a negative response.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: 못 (mot) is an adverb placed before a verb to express inability or failure to do something. It indicates that the action 'cannot' or 'could not' be performed, often due to external circumstances or lack of skill/opportunity. It is distinct from 안 (an), which expresses simple negation or unwillingness.
--- word ---
term: 잤는데
type: verb ending
breakdown: 자다 (jada) + -았- (at) + -는데 (neunde)
meaning: slept, but / even though I slept
notes: 자다 (jada) means 'to sleep'. -았- (at) is the past tense marker. -는데 (neunde) is a versatile connective ending that provides background information, introduces a contrasting idea ('but'), or sets the stage for the next statement. Here, it sets up a contrast: 'I couldn't sleep much, BUT (something else happened/is true)'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-는데/은데/ㄴ데
meaning: Provides background information, introduces a contrasting idea ('but'), or sets the stage for the next statement. It can also imply 'and' or 'so'.
example: 비가 오는데 우산이 없어요. (It's raining, but I don't have an umbrella.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: 못 V
meaning: Indicates inability or failure to perform an action. 'Cannot V' or 'could not V'.
example: 저는 한국말을 못 해요. (I cannot speak Korean.)
level: Beginner

term: 보통
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: usually, normally
notes: An adverb meaning 'usually' or 'normally'. It describes a typical or common occurrence. It can also be used as a noun meaning 'ordinary' or 'average'.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: See notes for segment 6.
--- word ---
term: 자고
type: verb ending
breakdown: 자다 (jada) + -고 (go)
meaning: sleep and
notes: 자다 (jada) means 'to sleep'. -고 (go) connects 'not sleeping' and 'waking up' in sequence. See notes for segment 0.
--- word ---
term: 일어났는데도
type: verb ending
breakdown: 일어나다 (ireonada) + -았- (at) + -는데도 (neundedo)
meaning: even though I woke up
notes: 일어나다 (ireonada) means 'to wake up' or 'to get up'. -았- (at) is the past tense marker. -는데도 (neundedo) is a compound ending combining -는데 (neunde, background/contrast) with -도 (do, even/also). meaning: it's refreshing / I feel refreshed

--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-는데도
meaning: Expresses a concession, meaning 'even though' or 'despite the fact that'. It highlights an unexpected result given the preceding condition.
example: 피곤한데도 계속 일했어요. (Even though I was tired, I kept working.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-아/어요 (해체)
meaning: The informal polite speech style, commonly used among friends, family, or in casual public settings like a Kpop idol's livestream. It's formed by adding -아요 (ayo) or -어요 (eoyo) to the verb/adjective stem, or -여요 (yeoyo) for 하다 verbs (which contracts to -해요).
example: 먹다 -> 먹어요 (I eat/am eating)
level: Beginner

=== [28:08] 뭔가 좀 뽀송하게 일어나는 느낌? ===
meaning: A feeling of waking up somewhat refreshed?
tone: Informal, reflective, questioning.
--- word ---
term: 뭔가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 무엇 (what) + -ㄴ가 (some, a certain)
meaning: something, somewhat
notes: Combination of '무엇' (what) and the suffix '-ㄴ가' (some, a certain), often used to mean 'something' or 'somewhat' in a vague sense. Can also be used as an adverb meaning 'somehow' or 'a little bit'.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a bit, somewhat
notes: Short for '조금' (a little). Used informally to soften requests or indicate a slight degree. Common in casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 뽀송하게
type: adverb
breakdown: 뽀송하다 (to be dry, fluffy, refreshed) + -게 (adverbializer)
meaning: refreshingly, dryly
notes: The adjective '뽀송하다' describes something as being dry, fluffy, or feeling refreshed and pleasant, often after washing or sleeping. The suffix '-게' turns an adjective into an adverb, meaning 'in a ... way' or 'to ...ly'.
--- word ---
term: 일어나는
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 일어나다 (to wake up, to rise) + -는 (present participle)
meaning: waking up
notes: The verb '일어나다' means 'to wake up' or 'to get up'. The present attributive ending '-는' attaches to verbs to modify a following noun, indicating an action happening in the present. Here, it modifies '느낌' (feeling).
--- word ---
term: 느낌
type: noun
breakdown: 느끼다 (to feel) + -ㅁ (nominalizer)
meaning: feeling
notes: Derived from the verb '느끼다' (to feel) by adding the nominalizing suffix '-ㅁ'. This creates a noun meaning 'a feeling' or 'a sense'. This is a common way to form nouns from verbs in Korean.
--- word ---
term: ?
type: punctuation
breakdown: 
meaning: question mark
notes: Indicates a question, even without a formal question ending, implying a rhetorical or self-addressed query.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -게
meaning: Turns an adjective into an adverb, or indicates the manner in which an action is performed. It means 'in a ... way' or 'to ...ly'.
example: 예쁘다 (to be pretty) -> 예쁘게 (prettily)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -는 (attributive form)
meaning: Used to describe a noun with a verb that is happening in the present tense. It functions like '-ing' in English when modifying a noun.
example: 책을 읽는 사람 (a person reading a book)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -ㅁ/음 (nominalizer)
meaning: Turns a verb into a noun, referring to the act or result of the verb. Often used in more formal or written contexts, but '느낌' is very common in spoken Korean.
example: 웃다 (to laugh) -> 웃음 (a laugh, laughter)
level: Intermediate

=== [28:11] 두통도 없고 ===
meaning: And no headaches either.

notes: A common noun for 'headache'.

breakdown: 
meaning: also, too, even

--- grammar ---

--- word ---

=== [28:18] 나는 그래도 믿는 편인 거 같아 ===
meaning: Even so, I think I'm the type to believe.
tone: Informal, reflective, expressing personal opinion.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + -는 (topic marker)
meaning: I (as for me)
notes: Combination of the informal pronoun '나' (I) and the topic marker '-는' (or '-은' after a consonant). It sets 'I' as the topic of the sentence, often implying a contrast or emphasis on the speaker's opinion.
--- word ---
term: 그래도
type: adverb
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so) + -아도 (even if)
meaning: even so, still, nevertheless
notes: Formed from '그렇다' (to be so) and the concessive ending '-아도' (even if). It means 'even so', 'nevertheless', or 'still', indicating that something happens despite a preceding condition or statement.
--- word ---
term: 믿는
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 믿다 (to believe) + -는 (present participle)
meaning: believing
notes: The verb '믿다' means 'to believe'. The present attributive ending '-는' modifies the following noun '편' (side/tendency), indicating the action of believing.
--- word ---
term: 편인
type: noun + copula + ending
breakdown: 편 (side, tendency, type) + -이다 (to be) + -ㄴ (attributive)
meaning: the type of, tending to be
notes: The noun '편' means 'side' or 'tendency'. When combined with an attributive verb or adjective and the copula '-이다', it forms the pattern '-는 편이다' or '-ㄴ/은 편이다', meaning 'to be the type of' or 'to tend to'. Here, the '-ㄴ' is an attributive form of the copula, modifying '거' (thing).
--- word ---
term: 거
type: noun
breakdown: 것 (thing, fact)
meaning: thing, fact
notes: A colloquial contraction of '것' (thing, fact). It is very commonly used in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 같다 (to be similar, to seem) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: seems, appears
notes: The adjective '같다' means 'to be similar' or 'to seem/appear'. When used with '-는 것 같다' or '-ㄴ/은 것 같다', it expresses a speaker's opinion, guess, or observation, meaning 'it seems that...' or 'I think...'. The '-아' is an informal, casual ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -는 편이다 / Adjective + -ㄴ/은 편이다
meaning: Indicates that one tends to do something or is the type of person/thing that is a certain way. It expresses a general tendency or characteristic.
example: 나는 매운 음식을 잘 먹는 편이야. (I tend to eat spicy food well.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -는 것 같다 / Adjective + -ㄴ/은 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a speaker's opinion, conjecture, or observation, meaning 'it seems that...', 'I think...', or 'it appears to be...'.
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (It seems like it's raining.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: 것 -> 거 (contraction)
meaning: A common colloquial contraction of '것' (thing, fact, abstract concept) to '거' in informal speech.
example: 이것은 -> 이건 (This thing is...)
level: Beginner

=== [28:21] 나한텐 남양이 잘 맞다고 그랬었거든 ===
meaning: They said that a south-facing direction suits me well.
tone: Informal, recounting past information, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 나한텐

meaning: to me, for me
notes: A contraction of '나한테는'. '-한테' is a particle indicating the recipient of an action or the person for whom something is done, meaning 'to' or 'for'. The topic marker '-는' adds emphasis, often implying 'as for me' or 'in my case'.
--- word ---
term: 남양

breakdown: 
meaning: south-facing direction
notes: Refers to a 'south-facing direction', often considered auspicious in feng shui for good sunlight and energy flow. This relates directly to the '풍수지리' (feng shui) discussion.

term: 이

breakdown: 
meaning: subject marker
notes: The subject marker '-이' (or '-가' after a vowel) indicates the subject of the sentence. Here, '남양' is the subject that 'suits' (맞다).
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: An adverb meaning 'well' or 'skillfully'. It modifies the verb '맞다' (to suit).
--- word ---
term: 맞다고
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 맞다 (to suit, to fit) + -다고 (indirect quotation)
meaning: said it suits
notes: The verb '맞다' means 'to suit', 'to fit', or 'to be correct'. The ending '-다고' is used for indirect quotation of a declarative statement, meaning 'said that...' or 'stated that...'. The verb '하다' (to say) is omitted, which is common in casual speech.
--- word ---
term: 그랬었거든
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so) + -었었- (past perfective/recollection) + -거든 (reason/explanation)
meaning: they said (it was so), you know (because it was so)
notes: This is a complex ending. '그렇다' means 'to be so'. '-었었-' is a past perfective or recollective marker, indicating an action or state that happened in the past and is now being recalled. '-거든' is a conjunctive ending that provides a reason or background information, often implying 'because, you know' or 'if (as a condition for advice)'. Here, it explains why she believes in feng shui.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -한테(는)
meaning: Indicates the recipient of an action ('to someone') or the person for whom something is done ('for someone'). Adding '-는' emphasizes the noun as a topic.
example: 친구한테 편지를 썼어. (I wrote a letter to my friend.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -다고 (하다)
meaning: Used for indirect quotation of a declarative statement. It means 'said that...' or 'stated that...'. The '하다' (to say) is often omitted in casual speech.
example: 내일 비가 온다고 했어요. (They said it would rain tomorrow.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -었었-
meaning: Indicates a past action or state that occurred and was completed in the more distant past, often with a sense of recollection or a past perfective nuance. It can also imply that the state no longer holds.
example: 어렸을 때 여기에 살았었어요. (I used to live here when I was young.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -거든
meaning: Provides a reason or background information for the preceding statement, often with a nuance of 'because, you know' or 'as you know'. It can also be used to state a condition for giving advice or a command.
example: 피곤하거든 일찍 자. (If you're tired, go to bed early.)
level: Intermediate

=== [28:24] 근데 진짜 남양이 잘 맞아 실제로도 ===

term: 근데
type: conjunction (contraction)
breakdown: 그런데 (but, however, by the way)
meaning: but, by the way
notes: A common colloquial contraction of '그런데'. It functions as a conjunction meaning 'but', 'however', or 'by the way', often used to introduce a new topic or a contrasting idea.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, truly
notes: An adverb meaning 'really' or 'truly'. It adds emphasis to the following verb or adjective. Can also be used as an adjective ('진짜 친구' - real friend) or an exclamation ('진짜?!' - Really?!).
--- word ---
term: 남양
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: south-facing direction
notes: As in segment 4, refers to a 'south-facing direction'.
--- word ---
term: 이
type: particle
breakdown: 
meaning: subject marker
notes: The subject marker '-이' indicates '남양' as the subject.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: An adverb meaning 'well', modifying '맞다' (to suit).
--- word ---
term: 맞아
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 맞다 (to suit, to fit) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: suits, fits
notes: The verb '맞다' means 'to suit' or 'to fit'. The '-아' is an informal, casual ending for declarative statements.
--- word ---
term: 실제로도
type: adverb + particle
breakdown: 실제로 (in reality, actually) + -도 (also, even)
meaning: even in reality, actually too

--- grammar ---

meaning: A common colloquial contraction of '그런데' (but, however, by the way) to '근데' in informal speech.
example: 그런데 말이야... -> 근데 말이야... (But, you know...)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adverb + -도
meaning: Attaches to adverbs to add the meaning of 'also', 'too', or 'even', emphasizing that the adverbial condition applies in addition to something else.
example: 빨리도 가네. (He's going fast too, / He's even going fast.)
level: Intermediate

=== [28:28] 내가 좀 편안함을 느끼는 거 같아 남양 쪽은 ===

meaning: a bit, somewhat
notes: Short for '조금' (a little). Used informally to indicate a slight degree.

--- word ---
term: 느끼는
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 느끼다 (to feel) + -는 (present participle)
meaning: feeling
notes: The verb '느끼다' means 'to feel'. The present attributive ending '-는' modifies the following noun '거' (thing), indicating the action of feeling.
--- word ---

meaning: thing, fact
notes: A colloquial contraction of '것' (thing, fact), commonly used in spoken Korean.

meaning: seems, appears
notes: The adjective '같다' means 'to be similar' or 'to seem/appear'. Used with '-는 것 같다' to express a speaker's opinion or observation, meaning 'it seems that...' or 'I think...'. The '-아' is an informal, casual ending.

breakdown: 
meaning: south-facing direction

--- grammar ---
pattern: 나 + -가 -> 내가 (contraction)
meaning: A common contraction of the informal pronoun '나' (I) with the subject marker '-가' to form '내가'.
example: 내가 할게. (I'll do it.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -는 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a speaker's opinion, conjecture, or observation about a present action, meaning 'it seems that...' or 'I think...'.
example: 그 사람이 오는 것 같아요. (It seems like that person is coming.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -ㅁ/음 (nominalizer)
meaning: Turns an adjective or verb into a noun, referring to the state or act. '편안함' (comfort) is a common example.
example: 아름답다 (to be beautiful) -> 아름다움 (beauty)
level: Intermediate

=== [28:45] 도넛으로 해놓은 거야 ===
meaning: (I) made it with donuts / set it up with donuts.
tone: Informal, explanatory, describing a setup or theme.
--- word ---
term: 도넛으로
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 도넛 + -(으)로
meaning: with donuts / by means of donuts
notes: -(으)로 is a particle indicating the instrument, means, or material used to perform an action. It attaches to nouns. Here, it implies 'using donuts' or 'making it a donut theme'.
--- word ---
term: 해놓은
type: verb + adnominal ending
breakdown: 하다 + -아/어 놓다 + -(으)ㄴ
meaning: that which was done and left (in that state)
notes: 해놓다 is a contraction of 하여 놓다, meaning 'to do something and leave it in that state.' The ending -(으)ㄴ is an adnominal ending that modifies the following noun (것, implied here), turning the verb phrase into a descriptive clause. It signifies a completed action that results in a continuing state. This is a casual form.
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: ending
breakdown: 것 + -이야
meaning: it is (that thing)
notes: A contraction of 것이야. This ending is used to explain or confirm a fact, often with a nuance of 'the thing is...' or 'it's because...'. It's an informal (반말, banmal) way to end a sentence, providing an explanation or background. The polite form would be 거예요 (geoyeyo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)로
meaning: Indicates the instrument, means, or material used for an action. Can also indicate direction or status.
example: 연필로 썼어요. (I wrote with a pencil.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어 놓다
meaning: Indicates that an action is completed and its result is maintained. It means 'to do something and leave it in that state' or 'to prepare something for later'.
example: 문을 열어 놓았어요. (I opened the door and left it open.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는/을 것(이다)
meaning: Nominalizes a clause, turning it into a noun phrase. Often used to explain or emphasize a fact, or to express a speaker's intention/plan. The '이다' part is often contracted to '이야/예요' in spoken Korean.
example: 이것은 제가 만든 거예요. (This is something I made.)
level: Beginner

=== [28:52] 지금 집은 전 집에 ===
meaning: My current house, compared to my previous house...
tone: Informal, transitional, setting up a comparison.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now / current
notes: Indicates the present time. Often used to modify nouns like '집' (house) to mean 'current house'. Compare with 이제 (ije), which means 'from now on' or 'at this point in time' (a new stage).
--- word ---
term: 집은
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 집 + -은
meaning: house (as topic)
notes: 집 means 'house' or 'home'. -은/는 is a topic particle, marking the subject of the sentence as the topic of discussion. It often implies a comparison or contrast with something else, which is evident in the following sentences.
--- word ---
term: 전
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: previous / former
notes: A prefix or determiner meaning 'previous' or 'former'. It's often used with nouns like '집' (house) or '직장' (workplace).
--- word ---
term: 집에
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 집 + -에
meaning: to/at the house (context for comparison)
notes: -에 is a locative particle indicating location ('at', 'in') or direction ('to'). Here, it's used in a comparative context, implying 'in the case of the previous house' or 'compared to the previous house'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: N은/는 N에
meaning: Used to set up a comparison or contrast between two nouns, often with the first N as the topic and the second N as a reference point for comparison. The sentence often continues with a conditional or comparative clause.
example: 서울은 부산에 비해 복잡해요. (Seoul is complicated compared to Busan.)
level: Intermediate

--- word ---
term: 생각보다
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 생각 + -보다
meaning: than (I) thought
notes: 생각 means 'thought' or 'idea'. -보다 is a comparative particle meaning 'than'. So, '생각보다' means 'more/less than expected/thought'.
--- word ---
term: 좋네
type: adjective ending
breakdown: 좋다 + -네
meaning: it's good! / how good!
notes: 좋다 means 'to be good'. -네 is an exclamatory ending used to express the speaker's sudden realization, surprise, or admiration about something they have just observed or experienced. It's an informal (반말, banmal) ending.
--- word ---
term: 하면서
type: verb ending
breakdown: 하다 + -면서
meaning: while saying / saying (and then...)
notes: 하다 means 'to do' or 'to say'. -면서 indicates that two actions occur simultaneously, or that the first action describes the manner of the second. Here, it implies 'while saying this thought' or 'saying this, and then (something else happened/I felt something else)'. The sentence trails off, suggesting an ongoing feeling or action.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -보다
meaning: A comparative particle meaning 'than'. It's used to compare two things.
example: 사과보다 배가 더 좋아요. (I like pears more than apples.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -네
meaning: An exclamatory ending used to express the speaker's sudden realization, surprise, or admiration about something they have just observed or experienced. It's informal.
example: 날씨가 좋네! (The weather is good!)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -면서
meaning: Indicates that two actions occur simultaneously, or that the first action describes the manner of the second. Can also mean 'while' or 'as'.
example: 밥을 먹으면서 TV를 봐요. (I watch TV while eating.)
level: Beginner

=== [29:18] 되게 빠르게 잘 적응을 하고 있어서 좀 ===
meaning: I'm adapting really quickly and well, so a bit...
tone: Informal, conversational, slightly reflective, trailing.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, very
notes: A colloquial and informal adverb, often used in spoken Korean as an alternative to 아주 (aju) or 정말 (jeongmal), both meaning 'very' or 'really'.
--- word ---
term: 빠르게
type: adverb
breakdown: 빠르다 + -게
meaning: quickly
notes: The suffix -게 (ge) is attached to the stem of an adjective (here, 빠르다 'to be fast') to transform it into an adverb (빠르게 'quickly').
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: A common adverb meaning 'well' or 'skillfully'.
--- word ---
term: 적응을
type: noun + object particle
breakdown: 적응 + -을
meaning: adaptation (as object)
notes: 적응 (jeogeung) means 'adaptation' or 'adjustment'. -을 (eul) is the object particle, indicating that 'adaptation' is the direct object of the verb '하고' (doing).
--- word ---
term: 하고
type: verb
breakdown: 하다 + -고
meaning: doing and
notes: 하다 (hada) means 'to do'. The ending -고 (go) connects clauses, indicating a sequence of actions or a continuous state. It's informal.
--- word ---
term: 있어서
type: verb
breakdown: 있다 + -어서
meaning: because I am
notes: 있다 (itda) means 'to be' or 'to exist'. The ending -어서 (eoseo) indicates a reason or cause, meaning 'because' or 'so'. Here, it implies 'because I am in the state of doing/adapting'.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a bit, a little
notes: A versatile adverb that can mean 'a little' or 'a bit', often used to soften a statement or request, or to indicate a slight degree.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective stem + -게
meaning: Transforms an adjective into an adverb, describing how an action is performed.
example: 예쁘다 (to be pretty) -> 예쁘게 (prettily).
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb stem + -고
meaning: Connects two or more clauses, indicating sequential actions ('and then') or simultaneous actions/states ('and').
example: 밥 먹고 학교에 갔어요. (I ate and then went to school.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective stem + -어서
meaning: Indicates the reason or cause for the following clause, meaning 'because' or 'so'. Cannot be used with past or future tense in the first clause.
example: 배고파서 밥을 먹었어요. (I ate because I was hungry.)
level: Beginner

=== [29:24] 내가 자신한테도 놀랬어 ===

meaning: Indicates that 'even to/for' the specified noun, something applies. -한테 is an informal dative particle, and -도 adds the meaning of 'also' or 'even'.
example: 친구한테도 말 안 했어. (I didn't even tell my friend.)
level: Beginner

breakdown: 
meaning: now, at this point

=== [29:57] 그냥 이렇게 아 나갈래 하고 내가 나간 건 아니니까 ===
meaning: It wasn't like I just thought, 'Oh, I'll leave,' and then I left.
tone: Informal, explanatory, clarifying.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, simply
notes: Indicates that an action is done without a particular reason, simply, or as is. It can also imply 'just because' or 'without thinking much.' Informal and very common in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: like this, in this way
notes: Derived from 이렇다 (ireota, 'to be like this'). It functions as an adverb meaning 'like this' or 'in this manner.' It refers to something close to the speaker or previously mentioned.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: oh, ah
notes: A common interjection expressing a range of emotions like realization, surprise, or a simple pause in thought. Here, it's like a verbalized thought.
--- word ---
term: 나갈래
type: verb ending
breakdown: 나가다 + -ㄹ래
meaning: I'll leave, I want to leave
notes: 나가다 (nagada) means 'to go out' or 'to leave.' -ㄹ래 (-llae) is an informal ending used to express the speaker's intention or will, or to ask about the listener's intention. It's very casual and often used among close friends or when talking to oneself. It can sound slightly demanding if used with someone of higher status.
--- word ---
term: 하고
type: particle
breakdown: 하다 + -고
meaning: saying, thinking
notes: Used after a quoted statement or thought, similar to 'saying that...' or 'thinking that....' It's the conjunctive form of 하다 (hada, 'to do' or 'to say'). Often used for direct or indirect quotation.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + 가
meaning: I (subject)
notes: 나 (na) is the informal first-person pronoun 'I.' 가 (ga) is the subject particle. This combination is used when 'I' is the subject of the sentence. The formal equivalent is 제가 (jega).
--- word ---
term: 나간
type: verb
breakdown: 나가다 + -ㄴ
meaning: left, went out (adjectival form)
notes: 나가다 (nagada) means 'to go out' or 'to leave.' -ㄴ (-n) is an adjectival ending that attaches to verbs to modify a following noun, indicating a past action. Here, it modifies '건' (geon, 'the fact/thing').
--- word ---
term: 건
type: contraction
breakdown: 것 + 은
meaning: the fact/thing (topic)
notes: Contraction of 것 (geot, 'thing' or 'fact') and the topic particle 은 (eun). It highlights 'the fact that I left' as the topic of the sentence. Very common in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 아니니까
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 아니다 + -니까
meaning: because it's not
notes: 아니다 (anida) means 'to not be' or 'to not be something.' -니까 (-nikka) is a conjunctive ending meaning 'because' or 'since.' It indicates a reason or cause for the preceding statement. It's often used when the reason is known to both speaker and listener, or when the speaker wants to emphasize the reason.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄹ래
meaning: Used to express the speaker's intention or will, or to ask about the listener's intention. It's a casual, informal ending.
example: 뭐 먹을래? (What do you want to eat?)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 하다
meaning: Used to quote someone's words or thoughts, meaning 'to say/think that...'. The '고' particle connects the quoted content to the verb '하다'.
example: 밥 먹고 싶다고 했어. (He said he wants to eat.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-ㄴ/은 것
meaning: Turns a verb phrase into a noun phrase, referring to 'the fact that...' or 'the thing that...'. -ㄴ is used after verbs ending in a vowel, -은 after consonants.
example: 내가 한 말 (the words I said)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V-니까
meaning: Indicates a reason or cause, meaning 'because' or 'since'. It's often used when the reason is a direct cause or when the speaker wants to emphasize the reason.
example: 비가 오니까 우산 가져가. (Since it's raining, take an umbrella.)
level: Beginner

=== [30:03] 그래서 아 너무 아쉽다 그래서 ===
